A multiparty homomorphic encryption approach to confidential federated Kaplan–Meier survival analysis
Authors: Narasimha Raghavan Veeraragavan, Svetlana Boudko, Jan Franz Nygård
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The proliferation of real-world health data enables multi-institutional survival studies, yet privacy constraints preclude centralizing sensitive records. We present a privacy-preserving federated Kaplan–Meier framework based on threshold CKKS (Cheon-Kim-Kim-Song) homomorphic encryption that supports approximate floating-point computation and encrypted aggregation of per-time-point counts while exposing only public outputs. Sites compute aligned at-risk and event tallies on a shared time grid and encrypt compact vectors; a coordinator aggregates ciphertexts; and a decryptor committee produces partial shares fused per block to recover aggregated plaintexts without releasing per-time-point tables. We prove correctness, stability, and slot-optimal vector packing, and derive scaling laws showing that communication grows linearly with the number of sites and predictably with the number of time points. Empirically, using synthetic breast-cancer data (N = 60,000) distributed across 500 sites, encrypted federated curves match the pooled oracle to numerical precision. In contrast, plaintext protocols permit trivial reconstruction by subtraction; our threshold-gated design precludes this attack under the stated threat model, enabling high-fidelity survival estimation with predictable overhead and substantially reduced privacy risk.

Introduction

Survival analysis is central to clinical and epidemiological research, yet privacy and governance constraints hinder centralizing sensitive records. Federated analytics (FA) alleviates this by keeping data in place while coordinating computation across institutions. We focus on the Kaplan–Meier (KM) estimator and adopt multiparty homomorphic encryption (HE) to protect intermediate statistics throughout the pipeline. A concise comparison of representative leveled fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) schemes appears in Table 1; we adopt CKKS with threshold decryption for its native support of approximate real arithmetic and efficient single-instruction multiple-data (SIMD) packing.Table 1Comparison of representative FHE schemes used in privacy-preserving analyticsSchemeArithmeticPrecisionBootstr.EfficiencyThresholdUse casesBFV13,14Integer (mod q)ExactOptional (leveled)ModerateYesPrivate queries; encrypted databasesBGV15Integer (mod q)ExactOptionalEfficient with batchingYesComplex integer analytics; encrypted MLCKKS6Real/complexApproximateOptionalHigh (SIMD packing)YesFloating-point analytics; encrypted MLFHEW16Boolean gatesExactRequiredFast per gateLimitedLightweight logic gatesTFHE17Boolean gatesExactRequired (per gate)Less efficient overallLimitedEncrypted control flow; logic circuitsCGGI18Boolean gatesExactRequiredFast per gateLimitedLUTs; conditional logic“Threshold” indicates widely available threshold decryption in mainstream libraries.

Comparison of representative FHE schemes used in privacy-preserving analytics

“Threshold” indicates widely available threshold decryption in mainstream libraries.

Existing federated KM systems have advanced practice, but four gaps remain that we address.

A key privacy gap (Gap 1) lies in subtraction-based reconstruction in plaintext two-round KM, which has rarely been quantified for overlapping, multi-site settings. We formalize and empirically demonstrate its practicality and then eliminate the channel by never releasing per-time-point tables.

An estimator-level theory gap (Gap 2) persists beyond cryptographic security proofs and microbenchmarks, with limited analysis of how approximate HE perturbs the KM estimator. To address this, we provide (i) correctness equalities for both plain and HE-exact settings, (ii) a CKKS perturbation bound with a uniform-convergence corollary, and (iii) an identifiability result showing that publishing survival alone reveals hazard ratios but not per-time totals or any per-site split.

A scaling-laws gap (Gap 3) exists in the form of communication and computational scaling laws specific to KM. These include the effects of packing choices, the stepwise dependence on the size of the time grid, and the linear dependence on the number of sites and decryptors, none of which are stated in a way that enables cost prediction a priori. We address this by deriving simple laws that align with observed behavior.

A packing design gap (Gap 4) remains despite the widespread use of CKKS packing, as we are not aware of a formal optimality statement for KM’s two streams (dt, nt) under add-only aggregation. We prove a slot-count lower bound and show that interleaved co-packing is optimal under this model.

Federated Kaplan–Meier (KM) systems to date fall into three main lines: (i) integer multiparty/threshold HE (e.g., BFV/BGV) exemplified by FAMHE1, which guarantees exact arithmetic but requires fixed-point encodings for real-valued workflows; (ii) approximate HE with CKKS2, which enables floating-point analytics but, to our knowledge, does not center on estimator-level guarantees for KM nor enforce output gating of per-time-point tables; and (iii) differential privacy (DP) approaches3,4, which are lightweight yet can degrade curve fidelity in sparse-event regimes. Secure multiparty computation (MPC) solutions 5 offer strong cryptographic assurances but typically entail heavier interaction.

Our work is distinct in four respects (Table 2). First, we couple threshold CKKS with output gating and return only the public survival outputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) (and optional bands), never the plaintext (nt, dt) tables, thereby closing the subtraction-based reconstruction channel we quantify. Representative federated KM systems across three families

Integer-HE (BGV/BFV), approximate HE (CKKS), and differential privacy (DP). Our framework differs by (i) threshold CKKS with output gating to the public survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) (no per-time-point tables), (ii) KM-specific estimator-level guarantees, and (iii) closed-form communication and computational scaling laws.

Second, we provide estimator-level theory specific to KM: plain/HE-exact equalities, a CKKS perturbation bound with a uniform-convergence corollary, and an identifiability result showing that publishing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) fixes hazard ratios but not counts. Third, we prove a packing optimality lemma for interleaving (nt, dt) under add-only aggregation and derive KM-specific communication and computational laws that predict costs and explain the observed interleaving speedups.

Fourth, we evaluate up to K = 500 sites on a large synthetic cohort (and up to 100 sites on NCCTG lung cancer in section S9 of the Supplementary Information; see Fig. S1 (Supplementary Information)-Fig. S3 (Supplementary Information)), showing numerical indistinguishability from the pooled oracle under both packings and an empirically eliminated reconstruction risk under threshold decryption.

Compared with prior CKKS-based systems, our framework combines threshold CKKS, output gating, estimator-level guarantees for Kaplan–Meier analysis, and explicit communication and computational scaling laws.

Our contributions are:

1. A complete multiparty CKKS framework for federated KM with threshold decryption and output gating that returns only public outputs (the KM curve and optional bands).

2. We ensure estimator-level guarantees: (i) plain federated KM equals the pooled oracle; (ii) exact HE reproduces the oracle; (iii) an explicit CKKS perturbation bound with uniform convergence as noise vanishes; and (iv) an identifiability proposition (hazards determined, counts not).

3. A packing optimality lemma for interleaving (nt, dt) and a computational scaling law that separates server additions, committee share generation, fusion, and the final KM pass.

4. We consider closed-form communication scaling laws for the two KM rounds and rules of thumb: encrypted-round bandwidth scales linearly with the number of sites and increases in predictable steps with the number of time points; decryption-share traffic scales linearly with the decryptor committee size.

5. An empirical study on a large synthetic cohort (up to 500 sites) and an NCCTG lung dataset (see Section S9 of the Supplementary Information; Fig. S1 (Supplementary Information)-Fig. S3 (Supplementary Information)) showing numerical indistinguishability from the pooled estimator under both packings and quantifying the reconstruction risk in plaintext vs. its removal under threshold HE.

We employ a two-round scheme: R1 discovers the global time grid by unifying local survival times (plaintext); R2 uploads encrypted per-time-point counts for aggregation and threshold decryption. We consider two packing modes: interleaved, which co-packs (nt, dt) pairs to minimize ciphertext count, and separate, which packs the two streams independently. Interleaving reduces bandwidth and decryption-share traffic whenever it fits more (nt, dt) pairs into the same slot budget; exact formulas and scaling laws appear in sections “Methods” and “Theoretical Results”. We evaluate deliberately non-IID (independent and identically distributed) client partitions via Dirichlet label splits (α = 0.2) and report all main metrics under both regimes, with figures cross-referenced in section “Results”.

Security and efficiency of HE schemes are well studied, but estimator-level KM results under CKKS are, to our knowledge, not formalized in prior federated work. We are not aware of (i) a KM-specific CKKS perturbation bound with a uniform-convergence corollary, (ii) an identifiability statement for publishing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) without counts in a federated setting, or (iii) a packing optimality proof tailored to KM’s add-only aggregation. Prior systems typically report empirical throughput or asymptotic crypto costs; our scaling laws are end-to-end for the KM workload and align with measured trends. Packing and communication are key practical determinants of add-only Kaplan–Meier deployment because they govern ciphertext count, uplink and share traffic, and runtime at scale. These quantities also inform deployment choices such as packing layout, committee size, and expected bandwidth.

Results

We evaluate the federated Kaplan–Meier (KM) pipeline along three axes: privacy, numerical fidelity, and system scalability, organized as six research questions (RQ1–RQ6). We adopt the following notation: K (number of clients), ∣T∣ (size of the event-time grid), n (CKKS ring degree; packing B = n/2 complex slots), ∣Q∣ (total modulus bitlength), R (committee size), and θ (decryption threshold in a θ-of-R scheme).

Unless stated otherwise, the pooled (centralized) KM curve serves as the oracle reference. For each metric, we report the mean and 95% confidence intervals (CIs) across repeated trials. An RQ is considered met when all pre-specified criteria in section “ Methods” are satisfied across the swept hyperparameters.

The six questions address privacy (RQ1), numerical and statistical fidelity (RQ2–RQ3), packing (RQ4), runtime (RQ5), and communication (RQ6), followed by analytical results.

Can a site practically reconstruct other sites’ contributions when per-time-point counts are exchanged in plaintext, and does our CKKS+threshold design remove that attack surface? (RQ1)

We simulate a federated KM setting with K ∈ {2, 10, 30, 50, 70, 100} sites under three overlap regimes (none, small, large) and two partitioning styles: IID and non-IID label splits (Dirichlet allocation with α = 0.2 over event labels to match section “Partitioning and heterogeneity”). In this reconstruction experiment, overlap refers to duplicate row copies shared across sites; these copies are treated as distinct records in the aggregate multiset. In the plaintext baseline, as commonly implemented in two-round KM, the coordinator broadcasts aggregated per-time-point counts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})},{d}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})})$$\end{document}(nt(Fed),dt(Fed)) to all sites, or sites can independently inspect them at the coordinator. An honest-but-curious site A then computes the residual\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${n}_{t}^{({\rm{others}})}={n}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})}-{n}_{t}^{(A)},\,{d}_{t}^{({\rm{others}})}={d}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})}-{d}_{t}^{(A)},$$\end{document}nt(others)=nt(Fed)−nt(A),dt(others)=dt(Fed)−dt(A),thereby attempting to recover the combined contributions of the remaining sites.

We evaluate both per-time-point event counts dt and numbers at risk nt across K and overlap regimes, reporting two consolidated 2 × 5 grids (mean with 95% CIs): one for IID (Fig. 1) and one for non-IID label splits (Fig. 2). Panels (E–H) report relative errors (lower is better):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\parallel \widehat{{\bf{d}}}-{\bf{d}}{\parallel }_{1}}{\parallel {\bf{d}}{\parallel }_{1}+{\varepsilon }_{0}},\,\frac{\parallel \widehat{{\bf{d}}}-{\bf{d}}{\parallel }_{\infty }}{\parallel {\bf{d}}{\parallel }_{\infty }+{\varepsilon }_{0}},\,\frac{\parallel \widehat{{\bf{n}}}-{\bf{n}}{\parallel }_{1}}{\parallel {\bf{n}}{\parallel }_{1}+{\varepsilon }_{0}},\,\frac{\parallel \widehat{{\bf{n}}}-{\bf{n}}{\parallel }_{\infty }}{\parallel {\bf{n}}{\parallel }_{\infty }+{\varepsilon }_{0}},$$\end{document}∥d^−d∥1∥d∥1+ε0,∥d^−d∥∞∥d∥∞+ε0,∥n^−n∥1∥n∥1+ε0,∥n^−n∥∞∥n∥∞+ε0,with ε0 = 10−12 to avoid division by zero and norms restricted to indices where denominators are positive. Panels (I–J) show exact-match rates\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{| T| }\mathop{\sum }\limits_{t}{\bf{1}}\{{\widehat{d}}_{t}={d}_{t}\},\,\frac{1}{| T| }\mathop{\sum }\limits_{t}{\bf{1}}\{{\widehat{n}}_{t}={n}_{t}\},$$\end{document}1∣T∣∑t1{d^t=dt},1∣T∣∑t1{n^t=nt},where higher values are worse for privacy. To contextualize heterogeneity, we also plot per-client event-rate distributions under IID vs. non-IID (two-panel figure, Fig. 3).Fig. 1Reconstruction metrics vs. number of sites K for IID data partitioning on the Synthetic Breast dataset.Ten metrics are shown: A F1 score for event support, B precision, C recall, D Jaccard index, E relative L1 error on dt, F relative L∞ error on dt, G relative L1 error on nt, H relative L∞ error on nt, I exact-match rate on dt, and J exact-match rate on nt. Per-site event-rate distributions for the synthetic breast dataset.

Violin plots show the distribution of event rates (#events/#rows) across sites under a IID partitioning and b label non-IID partitioning. Wider, skewed violins under non-IID indicate heterogeneous site contributions but do not prevent exact residualization when plaintext per-time-point counts are disclosed.

Across all K and overlap regimes, both IID and non-IID grids exhibit saturated support metrics (A)–(D) at 1.0, vanishing relative errors (E)–(H) near 0, and exact-match rates (I)–(J) at 1.0. In other words, once \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})},{d}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})})$$\end{document}(nt(Fed),dt(Fed)) are disclosed in plaintext, subtracting a site’s own \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{(A)},{d}_{t}^{(A)})$$\end{document}(nt(A),dt(A)) exactly recovers the combined “others” \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{({\rm{others}})},{d}_{t}^{({\rm{others}})})$$\end{document}(nt(others),dt(others)) on the common time grid, independent of K, overlap, or partition style. The flat lines with tight, often invisible, error bars reflect that this is an algebraic identity given exact counts and synchronized times.

The plaintext protocol reveals precisely the information the attacker needs. If the federated aggregator broadcasts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})},{d}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})})$$\end{document}(nt(Fed),dt(Fed)), as is typical in unencrypted two-round KM, any site can compute the residual and thereby learn the groupwise contributions of the remaining sites; with K = 2 this equals the other site exactly. This conclusion does not rely on IID assumptions. Non-IID label skew or overlap only change how counts are distributed across t, not the identity x(others) = x(Fed) − x(A) for x ∈ {n, d}.

The event-rate figure (Fig. 3) shows that IID splits produce tightly clustered per-client event shares, whereas non-IID splits produce broader, skewed distributions. These differences matter for inference from the survival curve alone, but they do not mitigate the plaintext subtraction attack, which operates directly on disclosed counts. Overlap increases redundancy in shared event times, but again does not impede exact residualization once totals are visible.

Our CKKS-based design removes the subtraction channel by withholding intermediate plaintext counts from sites and publishing only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), plus optional derived outputs such as confidence bands. Sites send encrypted packed streams; decryption requires a threshold of parties; and when the combiner runs off-server within the decryptor committee, the coordinator observes only ciphertexts. If the combiner is co-located with the coordinator, the coordinator may transiently view aggregated (nt, dt) to evaluate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), but per-time-point tables are not disclosed to sites. Because the attacker lacks \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})},{d}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})})$$\end{document}(nt(Fed),dt(Fed)) in plaintext, the subtraction step is unavailable, and reconstructing others’ counts from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) alone is under-determined without additional side information.

When per-time-point counts are exchanged in plaintext, reconstruction of other sites’ contributions is trivially exact in our experiments, with perfect support and exact matches across all K and partition styles. Encrypting those counts with CKKS and revealing only the final survival outputs removes the subtraction channel and thus the demonstrated privacy risk.

Do KM curves computed via our CKKS-based federated pipeline agree with the pooled (oracle) estimator as K grows? (RQ2)

For each K ∈ {2, 10, 30, 50, 70, 90, 100, 200, 300, 500}, we run the full protocol twice: once with interleaved packing and once with separate packing. The oracle curve is computed on the pooled plaintext data under identical time grids. We report means with 95% CIs across 10 repeated runs; interleaved and separate lines are overlaid in each panel.

We pre-specified equivalence metrics to assess numerical agreement between the HE pipeline and the pooled oracle across survival, RMST, band, and hazard spaces (Table 8). For the empirical hazard-space comparisons, we use the negative-log survival transform, defined as the negative natural logarithm of the estimated survival curve. Table 8 lists each metric, its formula, and the pass threshold used to declare “no practical difference.” Continuous-time definitions are used unless noted; thresholds are chosen to sit well above floating-point epsilon while remaining below CKKS rounding noise for our parameterization.

Figure 4 summarizes eight diagnostics in a 2 × 4 grid. Across all K, both HE configurations track the pooled oracle to within the numerical noise floor. Visually (Fig. 5), survival curves and bands are indistinguishable; numerically, all equivalence tests, including TAAE and IAE on S, absolute and relative RMST gaps, sup-Z and coverage, and hazard-based metrics, pass for every K and both packings. Packing choice affects efficiency, not accuracy: interleaving reduces communication and runtime, while fidelity remains unchanged.Survival. IAE(S) remains at the 10−8 scale (typically 1.3 × 10−8 to 2.6 × 10−8); TAAE(S) is at 10−12 scale (typically 3 × 10−12 to 6 × 10−12). Are the HE curves statistically equivalent to the pooled estimator within pre-specified tolerances? (RQ3)

We assess statistical equivalence using the same setup and figure as in Numerical Fidelity, namely the coverage and standardized sup-gap panels in Fig. 4. Both equivalence checks pass for 100% of replicates at every K. Interleaving does not change inference: HE-interleaved and HE-separate overlay within Monte Carlo noise.

Within our pre-specified tolerances, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is statistically equivalent to the pooled oracle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{oracle}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^oracle(t) for all K and packing choices; the HE pipeline preserves the statistical behavior of the KM estimator.

Do interleaved vs. non-interleaved CKKS packings change accuracy or only efficiency/communication? (RQ4)

We compare two encodings of the per-time-point statistics (dt, nt) under identical CKKS parameters and the same federated add–decrypt workflow: (i) interleaved, where one ciphertext stream co-packs [n1, d1, n2, d2,…], and (ii) separate, where distinct ciphertext streams pack [d1,…] and [n1,…]. Any accuracy differences therefore arise solely from packing layout. Figure 4 compares both packings under identical CKKS parameters.

Across all K tested (2–500), interleaved and separate packings are indistinguishable in accuracy within numerical noise: the two curves overlap in every panel of Fig. 4, their point estimates track across K, and their 95% CIs are nearly identical. IAE and TAAE remain at machine-precision levels in both modes; absolute and relative RMST differences are negligible; empirical coverage stays essentially 1.0; and the standardized sup-gap is ≈0. All pre-specified equivalence checks pass for both packings at every K.

Packing serves as an efficiency knob rather than an accuracy knob, since interleaving preserves numerical and statistical fidelity to the pooled oracle just as well as separate packing (Fig. 4). Choose interleaving to gain efficiency, not to trade off accuracy.

How does end-to-end runtime scale with the number of sites K for plain vs. HE modes? (RQ5)

Setup: All implementation measurements in this subsection use the deployed alignment grid Tall, so the packed length is L = ∣Tall∣. We measure the end-to-end wall-clock runtime of the experimental benchmark pipeline, including cryptographic setup, federated aggregation, threshold decryption, Kaplan-Meier evaluation, and associated implementation overhead. We compare: (i) a Plain pooled oracle (no encryption), (ii) HE with interleaved CKKS packing (co-packed slots), and (iii) HE with separate packing (distinct streams). All CKKS parameters, data, and code paths are identical across conditions except for packing. We sweep K ∈ {2, 10, 30, 50, 70, 90, 100, 200, 300, 500} and report means with 95% CIs.

End-to-end wall-clock runtime (seconds) is used as the evaluation metric (Fig. 6a). We also report the percentage speedup of interleaved over separate packing (Fig. 6b),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rm{speedup}}\,[ \% ]\,=\,100\cdot \frac{{W}_{{\rm{sep}}}-{W}_{{\rm{int}}}}{{W}_{{\rm{sep}}}}\,,$$\end{document}speedup[%]=100⋅Wsep−WintWsep,where Wsep and Wint denote the mean wall-clock runtimes under separate and interleaved packing, respectively.Fig. 6Computational scalability of HE-federated Kaplan–Meier on the Synthetic Breast dataset.a End-to-end runtime vs. number of sites K, showing approximately linear growth. Plain pooled oracle (gray) serves as a baseline reference, while HE with interleaved packing (orange) and non-interleaved/separate packing (blue) are shown with means and 95% confidence intervals. b Percentage speedup of interleaved over non-interleaved packing demonstrates increasing computational gains as K rises, plateauing at approximately 22% improvement for large-scale federations.

Interleaving accelerates compute without affecting accuracy. Interleaved packing is consistently faster, with the speedup increasing with K: about 1–2% at K = 2, approximately 10.6% at K = 10, approximately 16.5% at K = 30, and approximately 22% by K = 300–500. This matches the reduction in ciphertext counts and additions per round when (nt, dt) are co-packed (see Corollary 14).

Interleaving accelerates compute without affecting accuracy. Interleaved packing is consistently faster, with the speedup increasing with K: about 1–2% at K = 2, approximately 10.6% at K = 10, approximately 16.5% at K = 30, and approximately 22% by K = 300–500. This matches the reduction in ciphertext counts and additions per round when (nt, dt) are co-packed (see Corollary 14).

Pooled-oracle baseline. The pooled-oracle baseline remains in the millisecond regime (for example, approximately 5–160 ms from K = 2 to K = 500) since it omits encryption, transmission, and decryption. It serves as a lower bound rather than a deployable privacy-preserving alternative.

Pooled-oracle baseline. The pooled-oracle baseline remains in the millisecond regime (for example, approximately 5–160 ms from K = 2 to K = 500) since it omits encryption, transmission, and decryption. It serves as a lower bound rather than a deployable privacy-preserving alternative.

HE-federated KM is computationally predictable: end-to-end runtime scales roughly linearly with the number of sites, and interleaved packing yields a consistent 10–22% speedup at moderate-to-large K while maintaining identical accuracy (see Proposition 18).

How do uplink/downlink costs scale withK, the number of event-time points ∣T∣, and CKKS packing; and how large is the interleaving benefit? (RQ6)

This subsection reports formula-based communication simulations rather than measurements from the current implementation. Unless stated otherwise, these simulations instantiate the generic formulas with L = ∣T∣ to model an event-time-only packing variant. By contrast, the deployed pipeline packs over Tall, i.e., L = ∣Tall∣.

We evaluate communication using formula-based simulations implemented in our experimental evaluation. Each round, every site encrypts and uploads aligned per-time-point vectors over a packed grid of length L (in the communication simulations presented here, L = ∣T∣); the aggregator sums and triggers threshold decryption with R decryptors. We compare separate packing (two ciphertext streams for d• and n•) versus interleaved packing (co-packing (nt, dt) across slots). All runs use the same CKKS parameters (n = 16,384, ∣Q∣ ≈ 438 bits), giving B = 8192 complex slots and ciphertext size ct_bytes ≈ 1,794,048 bytes and partial-share size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathtt{share}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}\approx 897,024$$\end{document}share_bytes≈897,024 bytes.

All communication quantities are parameterized by the number of event-time points ∣T∣, consistent with the simulation code used to generate Figs. 7, 8, 9.Fig. 7Communication scaling.A Uplink vs. K with ∣T∣ = 1000 and R = 5; B Uplink vs. K with ∣T∣ = 10,000 and R = 5; C decryption-share uplink vs. R with K = 50 and ∣T∣ = 1000; D decryption-share uplink vs. R with K = 50 and ∣T∣ = 10,000.Fig. 8Communication scaling under CKKS packing.A HE uplink vs. K at fixed ∣T∣ = 5000 and R = 5; B HE uplink vs. ∣T∣ at fixed K = 50 and R = 5; C decryption-share uplink vs. R at fixed K = 50 and ∣T∣ = 5000; D interleaving benefit shown as HE uplink for non-interleaved and interleaved packing vs. ∣T∣, at fixed K = 50 and R = 5.Fig. 9HE-versus-plaintext uplink ratio.A Non-interleaved HE/plain uplink ratio vs. K at fixed ∣T∣ = 5000; B interleaved HE/plain uplink ratio vs. K at fixed ∣T∣ = 5000; C non-interleaved HE/plain uplink ratio vs. ∣T∣ at fixed K = 50; D interleaved HE/plain uplink ratio vs. ∣T∣ at fixed K = 50. Solid lines use the plaintext minimum-uplink denominator and dashed lines use the plaintext maximum-uplink denominator.

Let L: = ∣T∣ denote the packed vector length in the communication simulations. The number of ciphertexts per client is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{{\rm{int}}}=\left\lceil \frac{2L}{B}\right\rceil ,\,{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}=2\left\lceil \frac{L}{B}\right\rceil .$$\end{document}Mint=⌈2LB⌉,Msep=2⌈LB⌉.

Per-client uplink is M × ct_bytes, so total uplink scales linearly in K. Decryption-share traffic scales as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\times M\times {\mathtt{share}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}.$$\end{document}R×M×share_bytes.

The interleaving benefit is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rm{benefit}}=\frac{{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}}{{M}_{{\rm{int}}}},$$\end{document}benefit=MsepMint,which equals 2 × when L ≤ B/2, 1 × when both packings use the same number of ciphertexts, and intermediate values such as 4/3 × when (Msep, Mint) = (4, 3).

We also report the HE/Plain uplink ratio. The plaintext protocol uploads aligned counts over T using 2K∣T∣ scalars, whereas the HE protocol uploads ciphertexts. This ratio is therefore independent of K but depends on ∣T∣ through the stepwise behavior of M.

Figures 7–9 show communication scaling as functions of K, ∣T∣, and R.Linear scaling inK. For fixed ∣T∣, each client uploads a constant number of ciphertexts, so total uplink grows linearly in K.Stepwise scaling in ∣T∣. As ∣T∣ increases, M changes only when ∣T∣ crosses slot-capacity thresholds, producing staircase behavior in communication.Shares scale withR. Decryption-share traffic grows linearly in R and is independent of K.Interleaving reduces communication. The HE/Plain uplink ratio is constant in K but varies with ∣T∣, reflecting linear plaintext scaling versus stepwise HE scaling.

HE vs. plaintext. The HE/Plain uplink ratio is constant in K but varies with ∣T∣, reflecting linear plaintext scaling versus stepwise HE scaling.

In the current prototype, encrypted alignment may be performed over the full grid Tall. This affects measured byte counts in a live deployment but does not affect the formula-based communication results reported here, which are parameterized by ∣T∣.

Communication behavior follows directly from CKKS packing arithmetic. Total HE uplink scales linearly in K and stepwise in ∣T∣, while interleaving deterministically reduces communication by a factor Msep/Mint (up to 2 ×).

Theoretical results

We provide (i) model identities and basic invariance results supporting RQ2–RQ3, (ii) CKKS perturbation bounds explaining numerical behavior (RQ2–RQ3), (iii) identifiability from public outputs (privacy context for RQ1), (iv) packing optimality (RQ4), and (v) communication and computational complexity laws (RQ5–RQ6).

We use the notation of Table 4. Throughout, we assume a fixed centralized multiset of rows is horizontally partitioned among sites (IID or non-IID, with or without overlap); that all parties use a shared alignment grid Tall, derive the event-time subset T ⊆ Tall, and apply the same tie convention; and that the Kaplan–Meier (KM) product only changes at event times, so using event times to form T is sufficient for the estimator, whereas using Tall merely refines the computational grid by inserting zero-event time points. In theorem statements, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)$$\end{document}S^⋆(t) for the pooled Kaplan–Meier estimator computed from the aggregate counts on the event-time grid T; execution-mode-specific estimators carry explicit subscripts (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{S}}_{\mathrm{plain}},\,{\hat{S}}_{\mathrm{HE}}-\mathrm{exact},\,{\hat{S}}_{\mathrm{HE}}-\mathrm{ckks}$$\end{document}S^plain,S^HE−exact,S^HE−ckks). In the protocol section the released output is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t).

Model and basic identities

There are K sites, indexed by k ∈ {1,…,K}. Site k has a local set of unique observed survival times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall, including both event and censoring times. Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{{\rm{all}}}:=\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{k=1}^{K}{T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tall:=⋃k=1KTkalldenote the union of all unique survival times across sites. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{event}}}\subseteq {T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkevent⊆Tkall denote the unique event times at site k. The event-time grid used by the Kaplan–Meier estimator is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T:=\{t\in {T}_{\mathrm{all}}:\exists \,{\mathrm{an}}\,{\mathrm{even}}\,{\mathrm{tat}}\,t\}\,=\,\mathop{\bigcup }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{T}_{k}^{\mathrm{event}},$$\end{document}T:={t∈Tall:∃aneventatt}=∪k=1KTkevent,with cardinality ∣T∣.

The sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{event}}}$$\end{document}Tkevent and T are theoretical objects used in KM analysis and theorem statements. The implementation uploads only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall and constructs only Tall; the event-time subset T is handled implicitly because di = 0 at censoring-only time points and such factors do not alter the Kaplan–Meier product (Lemma 3).

For each ti ∈ T, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}^{(k)}$$\end{document}di(k) denote the number of events at site k occurring at time ti, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${n}_{i}^{(k)}$$\end{document}ni(k) denote the number at risk at site k just before ti. Global counts at ti are\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{d}_{i}^{(k)},\,{n}_{i}=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{n}_{i}^{(k)}.$$\end{document}di=∑k=1Kdi(k),ni=∑k=1Kni(k).The KM estimator on the pooled aggregate counts is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)=\mathop{\prod }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le t}\left(1-\frac{{d}_{i}}{{n}_{i}}\right),\,{n}_{i} > 0.$$\end{document}S^⋆(t)=∏ti≤t1−dini,ni>0.

We write hi: = di/ni for the discrete hazard at ti and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(t):=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le t}{h}_{i}$$\end{document}H(t):= ∑ti≤thifor the cumulative hazard evaluated on T.

When events and censorings occur at the same time ti, we adopt the standard Kaplan–Meier convention: the at-risk count ni is taken just beforeti, events are applied at ti, and censorings at ti affect only subsequent risk sets, not di/ni at ti.

Sites encrypt local contributions under a joint public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk. Conceptually, the server forms homomorphic sums of aligned local count vectors, and threshold fusion reconstructs packed plaintext blocks that decode to the aggregated counts. In the present implementation, the encrypted count vectors are aligned over Tall; entries corresponding to times in Tall⧹T have event count zero and therefore do not alter the Kaplan–Meier product. By Lemma 3, exact aggregation on Tall therefore yields the same KM estimator as exact aggregation on T.

CKKS with ring degree n packs B = n/2 complex slots per ciphertext; the RNS modulus chain is Q = ∏jqj with bitlength \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$| Q| ={\sum }_{j}{\log }_{2}{q}_{j}$$\end{document}∣Q∣=∑jlog2qj. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${q}_{\max }:=\mathop{\max }\limits_{j}{q}_{j}$$\end{document}qmax:=maxjqj for the largest RNS limb modulus. A coarse size model is ct_bytes ≈ 2n∣Q∣/8 per ciphertext and share_bytes ≈ n∣Q∣/8 per partial share.

Lemma 1

(Global grid invariance) For any horizontal partition of a fixed centralized multiset of rows (IID or non-IID, with or without overlap), the union of all unique observed times\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{{\rm{all}}}=\mathop{\bigcup }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tall=⋃k=1KTkallis invariant. The derived event-time grid\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=\{\,t\in {T}_{{\rm{all}}}:\exists \,{\rm{an}}\,{\rm{event}}\,{\rm{at}}t\}$$\end{document}T={t∈Tall:∃aneventatt}and its cardinality ∣T∣ are therefore also invariant.

Lemma 2

(Additivity of counts) For every ti ∈ T, if local counting rules are aligned to the same global grid and tie convention, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{d}_{i}^{(k)}\,\mathrm{and}\,{n}_{i}=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{n}_{i}^{(k)}.$$\end{document}di=∑k=1Kdi(k)andni=∑k=1Kni(k).

Lemma 3

(Grid refinement invariance) Let T be the set of event times and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T}\supseteq T$$\end{document}T~⊇T be any refinement obtained by inserting time points with zero events. With consistent tie conventions, the KM estimator evaluated on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T}$$\end{document}T~ equals the KM estimator on T at all t ∈ T.

Theorem 4

(Plain federated KM equals pooled oracle) Running R1–R2 in plaintext yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{plain}}}(t)\equiv {\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)$$\end{document}S^plain(t)≡S^⋆(t) for any partition of the same centralized multiset of rows.

Theorem 5

(Exact HE yields identical KM) If R2 uses exact homomorphic addition and threshold decryption returns the exact aggregated counts, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}-{\rm{exact}}(t)\equiv {\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)$$\end{document}S^HE−exact(t)≡S^⋆(t) for any partition. This remains true whether alignment is performed directly on T or on any refinement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T}\supseteq T$$\end{document}T~⊇T, including Tall.

For proofs of Lemmas 1–3 and Theorems 4–5, see Sections S1–S2 of the Supplementary Information.

Approximate HE (CKKS): perturbation of the KM product

We model CKKS and threshold outputs on the event-time grid as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{d}}_{i}={d}_{i}+\Delta {d}_{i}$$\end{document}d^i=di+Δdi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{n}}_{i}={n}_{i}+\Delta {n}_{i}$$\end{document}n^i=ni+Δni with deterministic error envelopes εd(i) ≥ ∣Δdi∣ and εn(i) ≥ ∣Δni∣. The bound below follows from a multiplicative product inequality applied to the ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}-{\rm{ckks}}(t)/{\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)$$\end{document}S^HE−ckks(t)/S^⋆(t).

Theorem 6

(CKKS perturbation bound) Assume 0 ≤ εn(i) < ni (so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{n}}_{i} > 0$$\end{document}n^i>0); define hi = di/ni and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${h}_{\max }=\mathop{\max }\limits_{{t}_{j}\le t}{h}_{j} < 1$$\end{document}hmax=maxtj≤thj<1. Set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\delta }_{i}\,:=\,\frac{{\varepsilon }_{d}(i)}{{n}_{i}-{\varepsilon }_{n}(i)}\,+\,\frac{{d}_{i}}{{n}_{i}}\cdot \frac{{\varepsilon }_{n}(i)}{\,{n}_{i}-{\varepsilon }_{n}(i)}.$$\end{document}δi:=εd(i)ni−εn(i)+dini⋅εn(i)ni−εn(i).Then, for all t,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\parallel {\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}-{\rm{ckks}}(t)-{\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)| \,\le {\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)\left(\exp \left(\frac{1}{1-{h}_{\max }}\mathop{\sum }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le t}{\delta }_{i}\right)-1\right).$$\end{document}∥S^HE−ckks(t)−S^⋆(t)∣≤S^⋆(t)exp11−hmax ∑ti≤tδi−1.

Remark 7

(Terminal jump with hi = 1) If di = ni at a terminal time point (so hi = 1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}^{\star }$$\end{document}S^⋆ drops to 0), the stated bound applies on indices with hj < 1. At and after the terminal jump, the trivial bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$| {\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}-{\rm{ckks}}(t)-{\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)| ={\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}-{\rm{ckks}}(t)$$\end{document}∣S^HE−ckks(t)−S^⋆(t)∣=S^HE−ckks(t) holds.

Corollary 8

(Uniform convergence on T) If εd(i), εn(i) → 0 for all ti ∈ T, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}-{\rm{ckks}}(t)\to {\widehat{S}}^{\star }(t)$$\end{document}S^HE−ckks(t)→S^⋆(t) uniformly on T.

For proofs, see Section S3 of the Supplementary Information.

Identifiability from public outputs

Proposition 9

(Hazards are identified; counts are not) Assume T contains the ordered event times and is known, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{S}(t_0)=1$$\end{document}S^(t0)=1. Then the per-time-point hazard ratios are determined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${h}_{i}\,=\,1-\frac{\widehat{S}({t}_{i})}{\widehat{S}({t}_{i-1})}, \qquad i=1,\ldots,m,\ \widehat{S}(t_{i-1})>0, \ \widehat{S}(t_0)=1.$$\end{document}hi=1−S^(ti)S^(ti−1),i=1,…,m,S^(ti−1)>0,S^(t0)=1.However, without additional side information (e.g., initial at-risk size, censoring schedule), the integer pairs (di, ni) consistent with these ratios are not unique; there exist infinitely many real-valued (di, ni) solutions and, typically, multiple integer completions. Hence publishing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{S}(t)$$\end{document}S^(t) alone reveals di/ni but not (di, ni) nor any per-site split.

For proofs, see Section S4 of the Supplementary Information.

Noise envelopes and utility under a simple model

We separate exact identities above from implementation-dependent error scaling. The following assumption captures our add-only pipeline and the empirical behavior of share fusion.

Assumption 1

(Add-only CKKS noise and share aggregation) (i) For a ciphertext contributed by site k using Mk ciphertext blocks at fixed scale, the encode–encrypt perturbation satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\parallel {\Delta }_{k}(\cdot )\parallel =O({M}_{k}\log {q}_{\max })$$\end{document}∥Δk(⋅)∥=O(Mklogqmax), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${q}_{\max }:=\mathop{\max }\limits_{\ell }{q}_{\ell }$$\end{document}qmax:=maxℓqℓ is the largest RNS limb modulus. (ii) Threshold-share fusion aggregates zero-mean, independent share errors across decryptors with variance proxy σ2, contributing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\sigma \sqrt{R})$$\end{document}O(σR) to each block’s error.

In our protocol Mk = M for all k since all sites pack over the same grid Tall; the propositions below are stated for the general case.

Proposition 10

(Utility envelope: coarse worst case) Under Assumption 1, there exist nonnegative εd(i), εn(i) with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varepsilon }_{d}(i),{\varepsilon }_{n}(i)\in O\,\left(\log ({q}_{\max })\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{M}_{k}\right)+O(\sigma \sqrt{R})$$\end{document}εd(i),εn(i)∈Olog(qmax)∑k=1KMk+O(σR)for which\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta S(t):=\left|{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{oracle}}(t)-{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{HE}}(t)\right|\le \mathop{\prod }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le t}\left(1-\frac{{d}_{i}}{{n}_{i}}\right)\cdot \mathop{\sum }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le t}\frac{O\,\left(\log ({q}_{\max })\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k}{M}_{k}\right)+O(\sigma \sqrt{R})}{\,{n}_{i}-{\varepsilon }_{n}(i)},$$\end{document}ΔS(t):=S^oracle(t)−S^HE(t)≤∏ti≤t1−dini⋅∑ti≤tOlog(qmax)∑kMk+O(σR)ni−εn(i),reducing to the large-ni form with denominator ni.

Proposition 11

(Bounded at-risk counts) If ni ≥ c > 0 for all ti, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta S(t)\le \frac{1}{c}\mathop{\sum }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le t}\frac{O\,\left(\log ({q}_{\max })\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k}{M}_{k}\right)+O(\sigma \sqrt{R})}{1-\left(O\,\left(\log ({q}_{\max }){\sum }_{k}{M}_{k}\right)+O(\sigma \sqrt{R})\right)/{n}_{i}},$$\end{document}ΔS(t)≤1c∑ti≤tOlog(qmax)∑kMk+O(σR)1−Olog(qmax)∑kMk+O(σR)/ni,which further reduces to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{c}{\sum }_{{t}_{i}\le t}\left(O\,\left(\log ({q}_{\max }){\sum }_{k}{M}_{k}\right)+O(\sigma \sqrt{R})\right)$$\end{document}1c∑ti≤tOlog(qmax)∑kMk+O(σR)for large ni.

Proposition 12

(Error scaling with client count) Under Assumption 1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varepsilon }_{{\rm{total}}}=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}O({M}_{k}\log {q}_{\max })+O(\sigma \sqrt{R}).$$\end{document}εtotal=∑k=1KO(Mklogqmax)+O(σR).

For proofs, see Section S5 of the Supplementary Information.

Packing optimality

Let B = n/2 be the CKKS slot budget and consider two aligned streams (d•, n•) of length L (in the implementation L = ∣Tall∣; in the communication simulations of section “Results”, L = ∣T∣).

Lemma 13

(Packing lower bound and interleaving optimality) Any packing that avoids cross-ciphertext multiplies and carries both streams over length L requires at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lceil \frac{2L}{B}\rceil$$\end{document}⌈2LB⌉ ciphertexts. Interleaved co-packing attains this bound with Mint = ⌈2L/B⌉, while separate packing uses Msep = 2⌈L/B⌉ ≥ ⌈2L/B⌉.

Proof idea: pigeonhole principle on total required slots; see Section S6 of the Supplementary Information.

Corollary 14

(Interleaving advantage) Let B = n/2 be the CKKS slot budget and let L denote the length of the two streams (d•, n•). Separate packing uses Msep = 2⌈L/B⌉ ciphertexts; interleaving uses Mint = ⌈2L/B⌉. Then Mint ≤ Msep with Msep − Mint ∈ {0, 1} and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}}{{M}_{{\rm{int}}}}\in [1,\,2].$$\end{document}MsepMint∈[1,2].More precisely: (i) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}}{{M}_{{\rm{int}}}}=2$$\end{document}MsepMint=2 when L ≤ B/2; (ii) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}}{{M}_{{\rm{int}}}}=\frac{2c+2}{2c+1}\in (1,4/3]$$\end{document}MsepMint=2c+22c+1∈(1,4/3] when L = cB + r with c≥1 and 0 < r ≤ B/2; and (iii) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}}{{M}_{{\rm{int}}}}=1$$\end{document}MsepMint=1 when L = cB + r with c ≥ 1 and B/2 < r < B.

For proofs, see Section S6 of the Supplementary Information.

Communication complexity

In R1 (plaintext), each site uploads its local unique observed-time set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall and the coordinator broadcasts the resulting global alignment grid Tall. In R2, the implementation uploads aligned at-risk and event counts over Tall, giving 2K∣Tall∣ scalars. An event-time-only variant that filters to the event-time subset T before uploading would use 2K∣T∣ scalars instead.

With CKKS packing (B = n/2 slots), a length-L vector packs into ⌈L/B⌉ ciphertexts. The formulas below use a generic packed length L: in the communication simulations (Figs. 7–9) L = ∣T∣; in the implementation L = ∣Tall∣.

Under separate packing,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{{\rm{sep}}}=2\left\lceil \frac{L}{B}\right\rceil ,$$\end{document}Msep=2⌈LB⌉,and under interleaved packing,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{{\rm{int}}}=\left\lceil \frac{2L}{B}\right\rceil .$$\end{document}Mint=⌈2LB⌉.

Theorem 15

(Communication: implemented plain federated KM) In the implemented protocol: R1 uplink: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\sum }_{k=1}^{K}| {T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}|$$\end{document}∑k=1K∣Tkall∣ scalars;R1 downlink: K ∣Tall∣ scalars;R2 uplink: 2K ∣Tall∣ scalars. In an event-time-only variant where R2 counts are aligned over T instead of Tall, R2 uplink reduces to 2K ∣T∣ scalars.

Theorem 16

(Communication: CKKS + threshold) If each client uploads M ∈ {Msep, Mint} ciphertexts in R2 (the value of M is identical across sites since all pack over the same global alignment grid Tall), the total R2 uplink is KM ciphertexts. Threshold decryption with a committee of size R requires RM partial shares, where M ∈ {Msep, Mint} is the number of aggregated blocks to be decrypted. Equivalently,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{{\rm{int}}}=\left\lceil \frac{2L}{B}\right\rceil ,\,{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}=2\left\lceil \frac{L}{B}\right\rceil ,$$\end{document}Mint=⌈2LB⌉,Msep=2⌈LB⌉,where L is the packed grid length.

Corollary 17

(Scaling laws) Implemented plain: Θ(K) for fixed ∣Tall∣ and Θ(∣Tall∣) for fixed K; in an event-time-only variant, the same asymptotics hold with ∣T∣ replacing ∣Tall∣.CKKS: R2 uplink is linear in K and in ⌈L/B⌉; share traffic is linear in R and in ⌈L/B⌉, where L is the packed grid length. Interleaving satisfies ⌈2L/B⌉ ≤ 2⌈L/B⌉.

The communication simulations in section “Results” instantiate L = ∣T∣; the implementation uses L = ∣Tall∣, and the same formulas apply.

For proofs, see Section S7 of the Supplementary Information.

Computational complexity

Proposition 18

(Computational cost law) The per-round work satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W({\rm{mode}})\,=\,\alpha K{M}_{\star }\,+\,\beta R{M}_{\star }\,+\,\gamma {M}_{\star }\,+\,{\delta }_{1}L\,+\,{\delta }_{2}| T| ,$$\end{document}W(mode)=αKM⋆+βRM⋆+γM⋆+δ1L+δ2∣T∣,where M⋆ = Mint or Msep depending on packing, L is the packed grid length, and α, β, γ, δ1, δ2 > 0 are mode-independent constants. In the current implementation, L = ∣Tall∣; in an event-time-only packing variant, L = ∣T∣.

The first term is homomorphic addition at the server; the second is partial-share creation by the committee; the third is fusion; the fourth captures packed-vector decode or alignment work on the packed grid; and the fifth is the Kaplan–Meier pass on the event-time grid. This mirrors the empirical near-linear trends in K and the interleaving advantage observed in section “Results”.

Corollary 19

Discussion

Our framework advances privacy-preserving federated Kaplan–Meier (KM) analysis by adopting CKKS-based multiparty HE with multiparty decryption and strict output gating. Unlike integer-only systems1 that require fixed-point coordination for real workloads, CKKS natively supports approximate arithmetic over real values. This capability enables faithful time-to-event analytics while constraining privacy risk in multi-institution collaborations2,6.

We provide estimator-level guarantees including equality results for plaintext and homomorphic settings, a CKKS perturbation bound with uniform convergence, and an identifiability statement. These results are complemented by Kaplan–Meier specific communication and computational scaling laws together with a packing optimality result based on interleaving (nt, dt). Empirically, encrypted federated curves are numerically indistinguishable from pooled oracle estimates across survival, RMST, band coverage, supremum gap, and hazard diagnostics over large synthetic cohorts (up to K = 500) and on the NCCTG lung dataset (see Section S9 of the Supplementary Information); Fig. S1 (Supplementary Information-Fig. S3 (Supplementary Information)). Interleaved and separate packings match in accuracy, while interleaving reduces ciphertext count and therefore bandwidth and runtime in the regimes predicted by the packing arithmetic. Although HE introduces overhead relative to plaintext computation, end-to-end performance remains feasible for moderate and large federations. The resource model developed here provides concrete byte and memory envelopes that support practical deployment planning.

We quantify a subtraction-based reconstruction attack in plaintext two-round Kaplan–Meier protocols. A site can recover the residual contributions of other participants by subtracting its own \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{(A)},{d}_{t}^{(A)})$$\end{document}(nt(A),dt(A)) from broadcast totals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({n}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})},{d}_{t}^{({\rm{Fed}})})$$\end{document}(nt(Fed),dt(Fed)). This leakage is exact when K = 2 and remains substantial for small overlapping coalitions. Our protocol removes this attack surface because per-time-point tables are never released. Only the survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) and optional confidence bands are revealed. By Proposition 9, publishing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) identifies per-time-point hazard ratios hi but does not determine integer totals (di, ni) or any per-site decomposition. Consequently the subtraction channel is closed even for honest-but-curious participants.

Estimator fidelity is established both theoretically and empirically. Plain federated Kaplan–Meier equals the pooled oracle (Theorem 4). Exact homomorphic evaluation reproduces the oracle (Theorem 5). Approximate homomorphic evaluation under CKKS yields a perturbation bound for the Kaplan–Meier product together with uniform convergence as noise approaches zero (Theorem 6, Corollary 8). Empirical evaluation confirms that survival errors, RMST differences, coverage diagnostics, and hazard metrics remain within predefined tolerances.

Communication and computation follow simple laws specialized to Kaplan–Meier estimation. With slot capacity B = n/2, interleaved packing requires Mint = ⌈2L/B⌉ ciphertexts and separate packing requires Msep = 2⌈L/B⌉, where L is the packed grid length. Encrypted uplink in Phase R2 is therefore linear in K and stepwise in L, while decryption share traffic is linear in committee size R. Computational effort decomposes into server additions Θ(M⋆K), share generation Θ(RM⋆), fusion Θ(M⋆), packed-grid decode or alignment work, and the final Kaplan–Meier pass Θ(∣T∣) on the event-time grid, where M⋆ ∈ {Mint, Msep}. These scaling laws match the empirical behavior observed in section “Results” (the implementation uses L = ∣Tall∣; the communication simulations use L = ∣T∣).

Our security posture reflects both the workload and the capabilities of current HE systems. We instantiate CKKS with parameters providing indistinguishability under chosen-plaintext attack (IND-CPA) under the RLWE assumption and enforce aggregate-only decryption through a multiparty committee. We do not claim IND-CCA2 security for CKKS. Instead we rely on operational safeguards including output gating, quorum-based multiparty decryption, authenticated transport, audit logging, and key rotation. These controls reduce practical risk in clinical data collaborations while preserving estimator fidelity.

In the present implementation, multiparty decryption shares are fused in an all-of-R configuration to recover aggregated counts required for Kaplan–Meier evaluation. If fusion is performed at the coordinator, the coordinator may transiently observe aggregated counts aligned over Tall, including zero-event entries at censoring-only times. However, the coordinator never observes per-site contributions and only the final survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is released to participating sites.

We deploy CKKS without decryption-time noise flooding in order to preserve numerical precision, achieving agreement with the pooled oracle at the 10−8 level. Instead of asserting IND-CPAD security, we constrain potential oracle exposure operationally through four mechanisms. First, multiparty decryption is used with θ = R. Second, policy gating ties each computation to a precommitted cohort and alignment grid Tall, from which the event-time grid T is derived. Third, the system exposes a restricted and non-adaptive interface with a bounded number of decryptions. Fourth, strict output gating ensures that only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) and optional derived public summaries are published; the per-time-point aggregate table is not released.

Resource usage follows directly from the packing arithmetic. With (n = 16384, ∣Q∣ ≈ 438bits), one ciphertext occupies approximately 1.794 MB and one partial share approximately 0.897 MB. With slot capacity B = 8192, the ciphertext counts (Mint, Msep) equal (1, 2) for packed length L = 1000, (2, 2) for L = 5000, and (3, 4) for L = 10,000. In the current implementation, L = ∣Tall∣; in the communication simulations reported in section “Results”, L = ∣T∣. These values determine the bandwidth envelopes reported in the Results. Reliability improvements such as idempotent uploads, bounded retry strategies, reassignment of the lead role when a decryptor times out, and chunked transfers improve resilience on unreliable networks without altering the cryptographic design.

Operational parameters that influence deployment include the slot capacity B = n/2, packing layout, committee size R, threshold θ, and the choice of packed grid length L. Interleaved packing is generally preferable because it preserves accuracy while minimizing ciphertext count. Fixing a modest committee size, such as R = 9 or R = 25, decouples share traffic from federation size K. Selecting a ring degree n that places L on a favorable packing step can reduce ciphertext counts more than it increases per-ciphertext size. Under add-only Kaplan–Meier computation at scale 240, numerical stability remains largely insensitive to the specific choices of n, R, and θ. Table 3 summarizes the recommended defaults.
