Notation used in the federated CKKS Kaplan–Meier protocol

Real-world multi-institutional deployments may experience network instability or slow participants (“stragglers”). The protocol structure naturally limits the impact of such delays. Phase B aggregation requires only a single ciphertext upload from each site, so network latency affects only the completion time of the aggregation round rather than its correctness. Because aggregation consists solely of homomorphic additions, the coordinator can accumulate ciphertexts incrementally as they arrive and defer Phase C until all uploads are complete.

Phase C decryption requires contributions from all R decryptors in the current all-of-R configuration (θ = R). In practice this stage can be made resilient to stragglers using standard distributed systems techniques such as bounded retry policies, checkpointing, and temporary lead reassignment among decryptors. A deployment requiring stronger liveness guarantees could instead adopt a θ-of-R configuration with θ < R, allowing fusion once a quorum of decryptors has responded. These operational mechanisms do not change the communication or security analysis presented in this work but improve robustness in heterogeneous network environments.

Secure multi-party computation (MPC) protocols provide an alternative framework for privacy-preserving survival analysis. In MPC-based Kaplan–Meier implementations, sites jointly compute the survival function using secret sharing and interactive protocols 7. While MPC offers strong cryptographic guarantees, it typically requires multiple rounds of interaction among participating sites, with the number of rounds depending on the multiplicative depth of the computation 8.

In contrast, the HE approach used here requires a single encrypted upload from each site (Phase B), followed by server-side homomorphic aggregation and a single round of partial decryption shares from the committee (Phase C). The total number of communication rounds is therefore small and does not grow with the number of participating sites. This simplifies deployment across institutions with heterogeneous connectivity.

Furthermore, CKKS natively supports approximate arithmetic over real values, which aligns well with statistical estimators such as Kaplan–Meier and restricted mean survival time (RMST). MPC-based solutions often require fixed-point representations and additional coordination to maintain numerical consistency across sites.

MPC-based approaches do offer complementary strengths. They can provide exact arithmetic with no approximation error, may support stronger security models that avoid assumptions related to approximate decryption in CKKS 9, and can be designed without a trusted aggregation coordinator. For settings where exact counts are required or where the coordinator cannot be assumed honest-but-curious, an MPC design may therefore be preferable.

For the federated survival analysis setting considered here, however, the CKKS approximation error on the survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) evaluated at event times remains below 10−8 across all experimental configurations (section “Results”), well within typical clinical reporting tolerances. Under the threat model adopted in section “Threat model, threshold, and key management”, and with output gating ensuring that only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is released to sites, the threshold HE design provides a practical balance between security, numerical fidelity, scalability, and operational simplicity for large federations up to K = 500 sites.

Several limitations remain. The protocol currently focuses on add-only encrypted aggregation for Kaplan–Meier estimation. Extensions to weighted estimators, interval censoring, or Cox-type models will require careful management of multiplicative depth or hybrid techniques combining HE with differential privacy. Additional directions include verifiable aggregation to protect against malicious coordinators, deployments supporting flexible θ-of-R thresholds under participant churn, and broader stress testing across heterogeneous institutional networks. Together these directions extend the foundation established here for secure and scalable multi-institution survival analysis.

Methods

Notation and preliminaries

Notation Throughout the paper, Tall denotes the global alignment grid of all distinct observed times, T ⊆ Tall denotes the event-time grid used by the Kaplan–Meier product, and L denotes a generic packed grid length. In the implementation, L = ∣Tall∣; in an event-time-only packing variant, L = ∣T∣. Each section that uses L states the instantiation explicitly.

For ciphertext layout, we write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{0}:=\lceil L/B\rceil ,\,{M}_{{\rm{int}}}:=\lceil 2L/B\rceil ,\,{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}:=2{M}_{0}.$$\end{document}M0:=⌈L/B⌉,Mint:=⌈2L/B⌉,Msep:=2M0.We let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}I denote the public ciphertext-index set induced by the chosen layout: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}=[{M}_{{\rm{int}}}]$$\end{document}I=[Mint] for interleaving and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}=[{M}_{0}]\times \{d,n\}$$\end{document}I=[M0]×{d,n} for separate packing. For interleaving, each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}ι∈I is a block index; for separate packing, each ι = (j, s) pairs a block index j ∈ [M0] with a stream label s ∈ {d, n}.

In the implementation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{R}}=[K]$$\end{document}R=[K] and hence R = K. When studying communication scaling, we also consider hypothetical settings with fixed committee size R independent of K.

Modern Learning With Errors (LWE) and Ring Learning With Errors (RLWE) based encryption schemes use noisy ciphertexts together with a linear-style decryption relation determined by a secret key. In threshold variants, the secret key is distributed into shares so that no single party can complete decryption alone; instead, each decryptor contributes a partial decryption share, and a combiner fuses a qualified set of shares to recover the plaintext. General constructions and security proofs for threshold LWE/RLWE-style encryption appear in Asharov et al.10 and Cramer et al.11.

The protocol notation in this work allows a general θ-of-R threshold among decryptors. In our implementation, however, we instantiate the strict all-of-R configuration θ = R using OpenFHE’s multiparty Lead/Main/Fusion APIs. Supporting a more general θ-of-R threshold is orthogonal to the Kaplan–Meier pipeline itself and depends on the underlying multiparty HE configuration rather than on the survival-analysis logic.

We use CKKS for approximate packed arithmetic on real-valued vectors. The later security analysis treats the protocol phase by phase. In particular, the Phase B ciphertext privacy claim is stated for the distributed threshold setting used here, where an adversary may already hold a strict subset of secret-key shares from the distributed key-generation phase. For that reason, the formal Phase B privacy theorem is stated under an explicit threshold-aware ciphertext privacy assumption rather than under vanilla single-key IND-CPA alone.

We distinguish between the full alignment grid Tall and the event-time grid T ⊆ Tall. The protocol and current implementation align encrypted counts on Tall, while the Kaplan–Meier estimator depends only on event times in T. Inserting time points with zero events does not change the estimator (Lemma 3), so this distinction preserves consistency between theory and implementation.

Federated KM with threshold CKKS: protocol and phases

We use CKKS6 with batching to support approximate real arithmetic on packed vectors. Our protocol organizes the federated Kaplan–Meier workflow into three phases that separate key setup, encrypted aggregation, and threshold decryption with output gating. The coordinator orchestrates the round structure and performs add-only homomorphic aggregation; sites compute local per-time Kaplan–Meier counts aligned to the shared global alignment grid Tall and encrypt them under a joint public key, while the final Kaplan–Meier product is evaluated on the event-time subset T ⊆ Tall; a decryptor committee contributes partial decryption shares that are fused into aggregate plaintexts from which the public survival output is computed. Protocol keywords grouped by phase

Phase A performs setup and time-grid construction; Phase B performs encrypted aggregation; Phase C performs multiparty decryption and output reveal. The keywords map to the abstract protocol tuple as follows: SETUP ↔ Phase A (InitCKKS through Union & Sort); PACK + ENC + AGG ↔ Phase B; PARTDEC ↔ MultipartyDecryptLead/Main; FUSE ↔ MultipartyDecryptFusion; REVEAL ↔ ReconstructCounts + ComputeKM + Reveal + Broadcast(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}$$\end{document}S^HE).

The protocol description and the security claims address different aspects of the system. In particular, Phase B uses CKKS encryption under a joint public key, but the formal privacy theorem for distributed encrypted aggregation is stated under the threshold-aware auxiliary-input ciphertext privacy assumption introduced in section “Security analysis by phase”, rather than being derived here directly from a standalone vanilla IND-CPA theorem for single-key CKKS.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{Params}}$$\end{document}Params denote the CKKS parameter set. The protocol\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Pi }_{{\rm{KM}}-{\rm{Fed}}-{\rm{CKKS}}}=\left({\rm{S}}{\rm{ETUP}},{\rm{P}}{\rm{ACK}},{\rm{E}}{\rm{NC}},{\rm{A}}{\rm{GG}},{\rm{P}}{\rm{ART}}{\rm{D}}{\rm{EC}},{\rm{F}}{\rm{USE}},{\rm{R}}{\rm{EVEAL}}\right)$$\end{document}ΠKM−Fed−CKKS=SETUP,PACK,ENC,AGG,PARTDEC,FUSE,REVEALcomprises: SETUP (Phase A), PACK+ENC+AGG (Phase B), and PARTDEC+FUSE+REVEAL (Phase C). The protocol notation allows a general θ-of-R threshold among decryptors, but in our implementation and experiments we instantiate the strict all-of-R setting θ = R. Only the released survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) (and optional derived summaries such as confidence bands, if computed) is published to sites.

Phase C: Multiparty Decryption & Output Gating

Phase A performs key initialization and construction of the global alignment grid. The coordinator initializes the CKKS context, fixes a deterministic site order π, and runs iterative multiparty key generation: the first site calls KEYGEN, and each subsequent site calls MULTIPARTYKEYGEN to incorporate its contribution into the joint public key. Each site retains its own secret-key share \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k}$$\end{document}skk; the coordinator learns only the resulting joint public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk. Measured in K, this phase performs one KEYGEN, K − 1 MULTIPARTYKEYGEN calls, and one broadcast.

In the same phase, each site uploads its local unique observed survival times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall (including both event and censoring times). The coordinator forms the global alignment grid by union:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{\mathrm{all}}={\mathrm{Sort}}\,\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{{\rm{k}}=1}^{{\rm{K}}}{T}_{k}^{\mathrm{all}}\right).$$\end{document}Tall=Sort∪k=1KTkall.The event-time subset T = {t ∈ Tall: ∃ aneventatt} is not constructed explicitly in the deployed pipeline; it is handled implicitly during aligned counting and KM evaluation, since di = 0 at censoring-only time points and such factors do not alter the Kaplan–Meier product (Lemma 3). The protocol operates over Tall for alignment and encrypted aggregation, while the Kaplan–Meier estimator depends only on the event-time subset T in theoretical statements.

The time-grid union runs in plaintext and reveals Tall by design. Deployments that require hiding local time sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall would need to replace the plaintext union with a private-set-union or related encrypted set-operation primitive.

Phase B performs encrypted aggregation of Kaplan–Meier counts. Given the shared alignment grid Tall and joint public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk, each site computes local event counts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}^{(k)}$$\end{document}di(k) and at-risk counts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${n}_{i}^{(k)}$$\end{document}ni(k) at each ti ∈ Tall, packs them into CKKS plaintext blocks, encrypts the packed vectors, and uploads the resulting ciphertexts. For non-event times, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}^{(k)}=0$$\end{document}di(k)=0, so inclusion of these indices does not affect the Kaplan–Meier product.

In the interleaved layout, slots are packed as [n1, d1, n2, d2, …], giving\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{\mathrm{int}}=\left\lceil \frac{2|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}{B}\right\rceil ,\,B=\frac{n}{2}.$$\end{document}Mint=⌈2|Tall|B⌉,B=n2.In the separate layout, the d• and n• streams are packed independently, giving\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{\mathrm{sep}}=2\left\lceil \frac{|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}{B}\right\rceil .$$\end{document}Msep=2⌈|Tall|B⌉.The coordinator performs only elementwise homomorphic addition to form aggregate ciphertext blocks. Server work is O(MK) homomorphic additions, and per-site uplink is Θ(M). No plaintext is revealed in this phase. The formal privacy guarantee for this phase is given later as an assumption-based ciphertext-content indistinguishability result in the distributed threshold setting.

Phase C performs multiparty decryption and output gating. The aggregated ciphertext blocks are sent to the decryptor committee. In the current implementation, all R decryptors participate, so fusion is all-of-R. For each block j, exactly one decryptor produces the lead share via MULTIPARTYDECRYPTLEAD, and the remaining decryptors produce main shares via MULTIPARTYDECRYPTMAIN. The combiner calls MULTIPARTYDECRYPTFUSION once per block to obtain plaintext block ptj, decodes the CKKS slots, and reconstructs the aggregate count table\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }.$$\end{document}{(ni,di)}i=1∣Tall∣.It then evaluates the Kaplan–Meier estimator over the event-time grid T:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)=\mathop{\prod }\limits_{\begin{array}{c}{t}_{i}\le t\\ {t}_{i}\in T\end{array}}\left(1-\frac{{d}_{i}}{{n}_{i}}\right).$$\end{document}S^HE(t)= ∏ti≤tti∈T1−dini.Because di = 0 at non-event times, evaluating the product over T is equivalent to evaluating it over Tall.

Only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), and optionally derived public summaries, is returned to sites; the aggregate count table is not released by the protocol interface. Costs scale as O(RM) partial-share computations, O(M) fusions, and O(∣T∣) for the Kaplan–Meier pass.

If the combiner is co-located with the coordinator, then the coordinator may transiently observe the aggregate plaintext counts (ni, di) needed to evaluate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t). This does not change what is released to sites, but it does enlarge the coordinator’s transient operational visibility and is therefore treated explicitly in the later deployment qualification of the security analysis.

Threat model, threshold, and key management

The system assumes a semi-honest coordinator and semi-honest sites. All parties follow the protocol as specified but may attempt to infer additional information from the transcripts they observe. Transport is protected by mutually authenticated, encrypted channels. Malicious deviations, adaptive corruption, and coordinator-site collusion are outside the present model and would require additional mechanisms and a separate analysis.

Sites perform distributed key generation as follows: the first site calls KEYGEN, and each subsequent site calls MULTIPARTYKEYGEN, producing a joint public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk while each site retains its own secret-key share \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k}$$\end{document}skk. The protocol notation allows a general θ-of-R decryption threshold, where θ is the minimum number of decryptors required for successful fusion. In our implementation and experiments we instantiate the strict all-of-R setting θ = R, meaning all decrypting parties contribute shares before decryption completes.

Phase A reveals public alignment metadata: each site discloses its local unique observed survival times, from which the coordinator forms the global alignment grid Tall. The event-time grid T is a theoretical subset of Tall and is not constructed explicitly in the implementation. The alignment grid is treated as non-sensitive and is broadcast to all sites.

The decryptor committee is intended to enforce an aggregate-only decryption policy bound to a precommitted cohort and alignment grid Tall, from which the event-time grid T is derived. In particular, the intended interface does not offer a general adaptive decryption oracle. Decryption is restricted to ciphertexts produced by the fixed aggregation pipeline, tied to the committed cohort and grid, and used only to recover the aggregate plaintext required for the Kaplan–Meier output. This restriction is important because our formal Phase C claim is output-relative: it characterizes what a corrupted decryptor learns relative to the fused plaintext and the released curve, rather than asserting a generic adaptive chosen-ciphertext guarantee for CKKS.

The following deployment controls are recommended:(i)time- or usage-based key rotation with fresh DKG;(ii)authorization logs binding each decryption request to the committed cohort and alignment grid Tall;(iii)attestation and audit of decryptor hosts; and(iv)mutually authenticated encrypted transport for all protocol traffic.

(i)time- or usage-based key rotation with fresh DKG;

time- or usage-based key rotation with fresh DKG;

(ii)authorization logs binding each decryption request to the committed cohort and alignment grid Tall;

authorization logs binding each decryption request to the committed cohort and alignment grid Tall;

(iii)attestation and audit of decryptor hosts; and

attestation and audit of decryptor hosts; and

(iv)mutually authenticated encrypted transport for all protocol traffic.

mutually authenticated encrypted transport for all protocol traffic.

If the combiner operates within the decryptor committee and is not co-located with the coordinator, then the coordinator never sees aggregate plaintext counts. If the combiner is co-located with the coordinator, then the coordinator may transiently observe the aggregate plaintext table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }$$\end{document}{(ni,di)}i=1∣Tall∣ required for Kaplan–Meier evaluation; however, only the final survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is released to sites.

Security model and claims

We analyze\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Pi }_{\mathrm{KM}-\mathrm{Fed}-\mathrm{CKKS}}$$\end{document}ΠKM−Fed−CKKSin a semi-honest (honest-but-curious), static adversarial model. Corruption sets are fixed before protocol execution begins, and corrupted parties follow the protocol faithfully while attempting to infer additional information from their observed transcripts. The view of a corrupted party includes its local input, its full random tape, and all messages it receives. Adaptive corruption and malicious deviations are outside the scope of this analysis.

The coordinator is modeled as a semi-honest, non-colluding aggregator: it performs homomorphic operations faithfully but may attempt to infer information from ciphertexts and metadata. Collusion between the coordinator and corrupted sites would enlarge the adversarial view and is excluded from the present model. Transport is assumed to be protected by mutually authenticated, encrypted channels.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}\subsetneq [K]$$\end{document}CS⊊[K] denote the set of corrupted sites. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{R}}\subseteq [K]$$\end{document}R⊆[K] denote the set of parties authorized to participate in threshold decryption, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=| {\mathcal{R}}|$$\end{document}R=∣R∣ and decryption threshold θ. In our implementation and experiments, every site participates in decryption, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{R}}=[K]$$\end{document}R=[K] and we instantiate the strict all-of-R setting θ = R. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}\subsetneq {\mathcal{R}}$$\end{document}CR⊊R denote the set of corrupted decryptors. The corruption sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}$$\end{document}CS and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}$$\end{document}CR are indexed over different protocol roles and may overlap.

Standard IND-CPA for CKKS governs ciphertext indistinguishability under chosen plaintext attack, but does not cover a setting in which an adversary can issue adaptive decryption queries. In principle, approximate decryption behavior can become relevant under stronger oracle models. Rather than claiming a generic adaptive decryption guarantee for CKKS, our protocol constrains the oracle surface operationally:(i)no single party can decrypt aggregated ciphertexts on its own;(ii)decryption is restricted to ciphertexts bound to a precommitted cohort and alignment grid Tall;(iii)the computation interface is fixed to add-only aggregation followed by one Kaplan–Meier evaluation pass; and(iv)only the released survival output \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is published to sites, while the aggregated plaintext table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }$$\end{document}{(ni,di)}i=1∣Tall∣ remains internal to the decryption workflow unless the deployment explicitly co-locates fusion with the coordinator.Accordingly, the formal Phase C claim below is simulation relative to the fused plaintext and released output, not a generic adaptive decryption security statement. The Kaplan–Meier estimator itself depends only on the event-time subset T ⊆ Tall, since di = 0 at censoring-only time points.

Our formal guarantees are phase-specific and transcript-based.(C1) Phase A setup privacy. The distributed key-generation transcript is simulatable from the corrupted parties’ local DKG state and the final global alignment grid Tall and event-time grid T. This does not hide the plaintext union steps beyond what is revealed by Tall and T themselves.(C2) Phase B encrypted aggregation privacy. The encrypted aggregation transcript is computationally indistinguishable from a simulation that does not use the honest sites’ local Kaplan–Meier count vectors, assuming threshold-aware auxiliary-input ciphertext privacy for the distributed CKKS layer. This is an assumption-based claim; we do not prove that assumption from RLWE for our specific threshold CKKS instantiation in this work.(C3) Phase C output-relative privacy. This is a protocol-level reduction to the underlying threshold decryption mechanism, not a derivation from RLWE alone.(C3) Output gating. The formal statements proved in section “Security analysis by phase” establish:(i)identical-distribution simulation for the DKG transcript in Phase A, relative to the exposed alignment grid Tall and event-time grid T;(ii)computational indistinguishability of the Phase B encrypted aggregation transcript from a zero-encryption simulation, under Assumption 2; and(iii)computational indistinguishability of the Phase C decryption transcript from a simulation relative to the fused plaintext and released output, under the threshold-decryption assumptions in Theorem 22.These are the precise claims supported by the proofs. In particular, the Phase B claim is not a standalone theorem about vanilla CKKS IND-CPA alone; it is a theorem about our distributed setting under Assumption 2. Likewise, the Phase C claim is output-relative and committee-relative, not a statement that the decryption phase hides the fused plaintext from committee members who legitimately participate in fusion.

Our security analysis focuses on confidentiality and transcript privacy in the semi-honest model. Authenticity and integrity are provided operationally by the deployment assumptions: mutually authenticated channels, per-message integrity protection, and signed metadata binding each decryption request to its cohort, alignment grid Tall, and ciphertext identifiers. We do not prove cryptographic soundness against an actively malicious coordinator or malicious sites in this work. Preventing malformed ciphertext uploads, replayed shares, or incorrect aggregation under malicious deviations would require additional mechanisms such as ciphertext validity checks, verifiable aggregation, and verifiable threshold decryption.

The guarantees above are transcript-based and do not eliminate all side channels. In particular:(i)the plaintext union step in Phase A reveals the global alignment grid Tall; the theoretical event-time grid T ⊆ Tall is not transmitted explicitly in the implementation but is included in the security model for theoretical generality;(ii)metadata such as ciphertext count, ciphertext size, upload timing, and committee size may reveal coarse-grained system information;(iii)the current analysis excludes coordinator-site collusion; and(iv)if fusion is co-located with the coordinator, then the coordinator may transiently observe the aggregate plaintext table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }$$\end{document}{(ni,di)}i=1∣Tall∣ needed to evaluate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), even though only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is released to sites.These limitations are reflected explicitly in the phase-wise theorems and in the deployment qualifications stated there.

Under the stated semi-honest, static model, our protocol provides: (i) setup privacy relative to the intentionally exposed alignment grid Tall and event-time grid T; (ii) assumption-based ciphertext content privacy during encrypted aggregation; and (iii) output-relative privacy during threshold decryption and release. Together, these characterize what the protocol hides, what it intentionally reveals, and what remains dependent on deployment choices and system-level controls.

Security analysis by phase

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi :={\Pi }_{{\rm{KM}}-{\rm{Fed}}-{\rm{CKKS}}}$$\end{document}Π:=ΠKM−Fed−CKKSdenote the protocol from section “Methods”. Let λ denote the security parameter throughout. We analyze the protocol phase by phase, since the three phases have different security goals and rely on different assumptions.

The guarantees proved below are intentionally phase-specific.(i)Phase A establishes privacy of the distributed key-generation transcript, but does not claim privacy for the plaintext time-grid union steps beyond what is revealed by the final global alignment grid Tall and event-time grid T.(ii)Phase B establishes computational indistinguishability of the encrypted aggregation transcript: honest-site ciphertext content is computationally hidden, subject to the public metadata exclusion stated in Theorem 21. The guarantee is assumption-based and relies on Assumption 2 for the distributed CKKS layer.(iii)Phase C establishes simulation relative to the fused plaintext and released output: the decryption transcript of a corrupted committee member is simulatable from the aggregate plaintext table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }$$\end{document}{(ni,di)}i=1∣Tall∣, the signed metadata \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{md}}$$\end{document}md, the released curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), and the adversary’s local state, under the threshold-decryption assumptions stated in Theorem 22. Informally, ordinary sites receiving only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) are exposed to less information than committee members, but this intuition is not formalized as a separate theorem.

Phase C establishes simulation relative to the fused plaintext and released output: the decryption transcript of a corrupted committee member is simulatable from the aggregate plaintext table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }$$\end{document}{(ni,di)}i=1∣Tall∣, the signed metadata \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{md}}$$\end{document}md, the released curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), and the adversary’s local state, under the threshold-decryption assumptions stated in Theorem 22. Informally, ordinary sites receiving only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) are exposed to less information than committee members, but this intuition is not formalized as a separate theorem.

Throughout this analysis we assume a semi-honest (honest-but-curious), static adversary: corruption sets are fixed before protocol execution begins, and corrupted parties follow the protocol faithfully while attempting to infer additional information from their observed transcripts. The adversarial view of a corrupted party includes its input, its full local random tape, and all received messages. Adaptive corruption and malicious deviations are outside the scope of this analysis.

The coordinator is modeled as a semi-honest, non-colluding aggregator: it performs homomorphic operations faithfully but may attempt to infer information from the ciphertexts and metadata it observes. Collusion between the coordinator and corrupted sites would enlarge the adversary’s view and is outside the present model.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}\subsetneq [K]$$\end{document}CS⊊[K] denote the set of corrupted sites. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{R}}\subseteq [K]$$\end{document}R⊆[K] denote the set of parties authorized to participate in threshold decryption; in our implementation every site participates, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{R}}=[K]$$\end{document}R=[K] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=| {\mathcal{R}}| =K$$\end{document}R=∣R∣=K. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}\subsetneq {\mathcal{R}}$$\end{document}CR⊊R denote the set of corrupted decryptors. The protocol supports a general θ-of-R threshold; our implementation uses θ = R. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}$$\end{document}CS and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}$$\end{document}CR are indexed over different protocol roles, they are treated as independent corruption sets even when they overlap. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}\cap {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}\ne {\rm{\varnothing }}$$\end{document}CS∩CR≠∅, the shared secret-key shares are carried as persistent adversary state across phases; the composition discussion below addresses this explicitly.

The simulators defined for the three phases may be composed into a single global simulator. The phases execute sequentially, and the inter-phase interfaces relevant to simulation consist only of public outputs: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathsf{pk}},{T}_{{\rm{all}}})$$\end{document}(pk,Tall) from Phase A and the ciphertext blocks from Phase B. Corrupted parties’ secret-key shares persist as auxiliary adversary state and are threaded unchanged through the composition; they do not alter the public inter-phase outputs to which the sequential composition argument applies.

Phase A consists of two substeps: (a) distributed key generation, which produces the joint public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk and local secret-key shares \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k}\}$$\end{document}{skk}; and (b) construction of the global alignment grid\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{{\rm{all}}}={\rm{Sort}}\left(\mathop{\bigcup }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}\right).$$\end{document}Tall=Sort⋃k=1KTkall.The event-time subset T = {t ∈ Tall: ∃ an even tat t} is a theoretical object; it is not constructed explicitly in the implementation. The first substep is data-independent; the second intentionally reveals the union of local observed-time sets.

Theorem 20

(Phase A setup privacy) Assume the distributed key-generation mechanism used in Π is correctly specified and data-independent: for any fixed corrupted-party state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k},{\rho }_{k}\}}_{k\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}}$$\end{document}{skk,ρk}k∈CS, the joint distribution of the DKG transcript and joint public key depends only on the parties’ key-generation randomness and secret-key material, not on any site’s plaintext dataset. Then there exists a PPT simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{A}$$\end{document}SA such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{A}\left({\mathsf{Params}},\,{\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k},{\rho }_{k}\}}_{k\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}},\,{T}_{{\rm{all}}},\,T\right)\,{=}_{d}\,{{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(A)}(\Pi ),$$\end{document}SAParams,{skk,ρk}k∈CS,Tall,T=dViewA(A)(Π),where = d denotes identical distribution.

Proof.

The simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{A}$$\end{document}SA operates as follows. For each honest party \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}$$\end{document}k∉CS, it samples fresh key-generation randomness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{\rho }}_{k}$$\end{document}ρ~k independently from the same distribution specified by the DKG protocol. It then runs the DKG protocol using the real corrupted-party inputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k},{\rho }_{k}\}}_{k\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}}$$\end{document}{skk,ρk}k∈CS and the freshly sampled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{\widetilde{\rho }}_{k}\}}_{k\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}}$$\end{document}{ρ~k}k∉CS, producing a simulated DKG transcript and joint public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\mathsf{pk}}}$$\end{document}pk~. It appends Tall and T.

By the data-independence assumption, the joint distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\rm{DKGtranscript}},{\mathsf{pk}})$$\end{document}(DKGtranscript,pk) depends only on key-generation randomness and secret-key material. In the real execution, the honest parties’ randomness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{\rho }_{k}\}}_{k\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}}$$\end{document}{ρk}k∉CS is drawn independently from the prescribed distribution; the simulator draws \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{\widetilde{\rho }}_{k}\}}_{k\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}}$$\end{document}{ρ~k}k∉CS from the identical distribution. Since the corrupted-party state is identical in both cases, and the honest randomness has the same distribution, the joint distribution of the DKG transcript, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\mathsf{pk}}},\,{T}_{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}pk~,Tall, and T in the simulation is identical to that of the DKG transcript, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}},\,{T}_{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}pk,Tall, and T in a real execution.

Theorem 20 does not claim privacy for the individual sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall beyond what Tall reveals. In the present implementation the union is computed in plaintext; timepoints are treated as non-sensitive metadata. If a stricter deployment requires hiding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall, the union step must be replaced by a private-set-union protocol analyzed separately.

Each site k computes local Kaplan–Meier count pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${({d}_{i}^{(k)},{n}_{i}^{(k)})}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| }$$\end{document}(di(k),ni(k))i=1∣Tall∣ over the shared alignment grid Tall, packs them into CKKS plaintext vectors, encrypts them under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk, and uploads the resulting ciphertext blocks. Sites with no event at a given grid point contribute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}^{(k)}=0$$\end{document}di(k)=0 for that position. The coordinator performs only homomorphic addition on received ciphertexts.

Let B denote the number of plaintext slots in a single CKKS ciphertext, a public parameter (with B = n/2 for CKKS). Each site k packs its count pairs over the alignment grid Tall into CKKS plaintext vectors using a fixed, publicly known packing algorithm. With packed grid length L = ∣Tall∣, the public ciphertext-index set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}I has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$| {\mathcal{I}}| ={M}_{{\rm{int}}}=\lceil 2L/B\rceil$$\end{document}∣I∣=Mint=⌈2L/B⌉ for interleaving and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$| {\mathcal{I}}| ={M}_{{\rm{sep}}}=2\lceil L/B\rceil$$\end{document}∣I∣=Msep=2⌈L/B⌉ for separate packing. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\bf{z}}}_{\iota }^{(k)}\in {{\mathbb{R}}}^{B}$$\end{document}zι(k)∈RB for the packed vector of site k at index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}ι∈I. Since B, L, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}I are public, all ciphertext block dimensions are derivable without accessing any site’s private data. Because every site packs counts over the same global alignment grid Tall, the index set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}I is identical for all sites. The proof below is stated for a generic ι; for interleaving each ι is a block index, while for separate packing each ι = (j, s) pairs a block index j ∈ [M0] with a stream label s ∈ {d, n}.

Standard IND-CPA for public-key encryption is stated for adversaries that see the public key but not any secret-key material. In our distributed setting, a Phase B adversary may additionally hold a strict subset of secret-key shares produced by the DKG. To make the security claim precise, we isolate the following assumption for the distributed CKKS layer used by ΠKM − Fed − CKKS.

Remark. Assumption 2 is stronger than standard IND-CPA because the distinguisher is additionally given a strict subset of secret-key shares and auxiliary ciphertexts. We therefore state it explicitly as an assumption on the distributed CKKS layer used by our protocol; establishing it formally for a specific threshold CKKS instantiation is outside the scope of this work.

Theorem 21

(Transcript privacy of Phase B encrypted aggregation) Assume Assumption 2. Then there exists a PPT simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}$$\end{document}SB such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}\,\left({\mathsf{Params}},\,{\{{({d}_{i}^{(k)},{n}_{i}^{(k)})}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| },{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k},{\rho }_{k}^{{\rm{enc}}}\}}_{k\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}},\,{\mathsf{pk}}\right)\,\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}\,{{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(B)}(\Pi ).$$\end{document}SBParams,{(di(k),ni(k))i=1∣Tall∣,skk,ρkenc}k∈CS,pk≈cViewA(B)(Π).That is, the Phase B encrypted aggregation transcript is computationally indistinguishable from a simulation that does not use the honest sites’ local Kaplan–Meier count vectors. This guarantee covers ciphertext content privacy only; public metadata such as the common block count M, ciphertext sizes, and upload timing is excluded.

Proof.

We construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}$$\end{document}SB and prove indistinguishability by a standard sequence-of-games argument.

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{Params}}$$\end{document}Params, the corrupted sites’ inputs, corrupted secret-key shares, corrupted encryption randomness, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk, the simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}$$\end{document}SB proceeds as follows. For notational simplicity, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho }_{k}^{{\rm{enc}}}$$\end{document}ρkenc denotes the full encryption random tape of corrupted site k, whose per-block components are written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho }_{k,j}^{{\rm{enc}}}$$\end{document}ρk,jenc when individual ciphertext blocks are referenced.

(i) It sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\mathsf{pk}}}:={\mathsf{pk}}$$\end{document}pk~:=pk.

(ii) For each corrupted site \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}$$\end{document}k∈CS, it computes the packed plaintext vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\bf{z}}}_{j}^{(k)}$$\end{document}zj(k) from the given corrupted inputs and encrypts them using the given randomness:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{{\mathsf{ct}}}}_{j}^{(k)}:={E}_{\hat{{\mathsf{pk}}}}({{\bf{z}}}_{j}^{(k)};\,{\rho }_{k,j}^{{\rm{enc}}}),\,j=1,\ldots ,M.$$\end{document}ct~j(k):=Epk~zj(k);ρk,jenc,j=1,…,M.Hence the corrupted sites’ ciphertexts are reproduced exactly.

(iii) Since every site packs over the same public alignment grid Tall, each site uploads exactly M ciphertext blocks. For each honest site \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}$$\end{document}k∉CS and each j = 1, …, M, the simulator outputs\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{{\mathsf{ct}}}}_{j}^{(k)}\leftarrow {E}_{\tilde{{\mathsf{pk}}}}({{\bf{0}}}_{B}),$$\end{document}ct~j(k)←Epk~(0B),using fresh randomness.

(iv) For each block index j, it computes the aggregate ciphertext by CKKS homomorphic addition:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{{\mathsf{ct}}}}_{\Sigma ,j}=\mathop{\bigoplus }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{\overline{{\mathsf{ct}}}}_{j}^{(k)}.$$\end{document}ct~Σ,j=⨁k=1Kct~j(k).

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=(K-| {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}| )\cdot M$$\end{document}N=(K−∣CS∣)⋅Mbe the total number of honest-site ciphertext blocks. Fix an arbitrary but deterministic ordering of these honest blocks, and define hybrids\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H}^{(0)},{H}^{(1)},\ldots ,{H}^{(N)},$$\end{document}H(0),H(1),…,H(N),where:H(0) is the real Phase B transcript;in H(ℓ), the first ℓ honest ciphertext blocks in the fixed ordering are replaced by encryptions of 0B, while all remaining honest blocks are real encryptions; andH(N) is exactly the simulator output.

H(0) is the real Phase B transcript;

H(0) is the real Phase B transcript;

in H(ℓ), the first ℓ honest ciphertext blocks in the fixed ordering are replaced by encryptions of 0B, while all remaining honest blocks are real encryptions; and

in H(ℓ), the first ℓ honest ciphertext blocks in the fixed ordering are replaced by encryptions of 0B, while all remaining honest blocks are real encryptions; and

H(N) is exactly the simulator output.

H(N) is exactly the simulator output.

The corrupted sites’ inputs, secret-key shares, encryption randomness, and ciphertexts are identical in every hybrid. The only changes occur in the honest ciphertext blocks.

Now consider adjacent hybrids H(ℓ−1) and H(ℓ). They differ in exactly one honest ciphertext block, say block (k, j). In H(ℓ−1) that block is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{{\mathsf{pk}}}\left({{\bf{z}}}_{j}^{(k)}\right),$$\end{document}Epkzj(k),whereas in H(ℓ) it is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{{\mathsf{pk}}}({{\bf{0}}}_{B}).$$\end{document}Epk(0B).All other ciphertexts, including all corrupted-site ciphertexts and the other honest-site ciphertexts, are unchanged. Therefore they may be treated as auxiliary input in Assumption 2, together with the corrupted secret-key shares.

If some PPT distinguisher separated H(ℓ−1) from H(ℓ) with non-negligible advantage, then this would violate Assumption 2 for the plaintext pair\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({{\bf{z}}}_{j}^{(k)},\,{{\bf{0}}}_{B}\right),$$\end{document}zj(k),0B,with the unchanged ciphertext blocks and corrupted shares supplied as auxiliary input. Hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H}^{(\ell -1)}\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}{H}^{(\ell )}\,{\rm{forevery}}\ell =1,\ldots ,N.$$\end{document}H(ℓ−1)≈cH(ℓ)foreveryℓ=1,…,N.

Because N = poly(λ), a standard telescoping hybrid argument gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H}^{(0)}\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}{H}^{(N)}.$$\end{document}H(0)≈cH(N).

Finally, the aggregate ciphertexts are deterministic efficient functions of the uploaded ciphertext blocks. Computational indistinguishability is preserved under such post-processing, so the aggregate ciphertext components remain computationally indistinguishable as well.

Since H(N) is exactly the transcript output by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}$$\end{document}SB, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}\left({\mathsf{Params}},\,{\{{({d}_{i}^{(k)},{n}_{i}^{(k)})}_{i=1}^{| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| },{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{k},{\rho }_{k}^{{\rm{enc}}}\}}_{k\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}},\,{\mathsf{pk}}\right)\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}{{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(B)}(\Pi ),$$\end{document}SBParams,{(di(k),ni(k))i=1∣Tall∣,skk,ρkenc}k∈CS,pk≈cViewA(B)(Π),as claimed.

The above argument covers ciphertext content privacy. The block count M, upload timing, and ciphertext sizes are observable metadata. Because all sites pack over the same global alignment grid Tall, the block count M is identical across sites and reveals only ∣Tall∣, which is already public from Phase A. Upload timing may reveal participation ordering and is outside the cryptographic guarantee; it should be addressed at the network layer if needed.

Phase C releases a public result, so the security goal is not full hiding but simulation relative to the information legitimately revealed.

The following Phase C claim is output-relative: it simulates the view of a corrupted decryptor coalition given the aggregate plaintext table recovered inside the decryption committee and the released curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t). It does not assert that a subthreshold coalition can recover the aggregate table on its own. The adversary is modeled relative to the internal decryption outcome available within the committee execution. Informally, ordinary sites receiving only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) are exposed to less information than committee members, but this is not formalized as a separate theorem. The theorem does not claim security against adaptive decryption queries, which are operationally excluded by policy controls in section “Threat model, threshold, and key management”. The simulated leakage for Phase C is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{L}}}_{C}=({\mathsf{md}},P,{\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t))$$\end{document}LC=(md,P,S^HE(t)), together with the corrupted parties’ local keys and local randomness.

The decryption request is accompanied by a signed metadata blob \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{md}}$$\end{document}md: a coordinator-authenticated statement attesting the cohort identifier, the committed alignment grid Tall, and the ciphertext block identifiers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}}$$\end{document}{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I. The signature is produced under a signing key provisioned separately from the CKKS setup, for example via out-of-band PKI. The Phase C adversarial view includes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{md}}$$\end{document}md directly; the simulator receives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{md}}$$\end{document}md as explicit input and copies it verbatim, requiring no signing capability.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(C)}(\Pi )$$\end{document}ViewA(C)(Π) denote the Phase C view of a PPT adversary corrupting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}\subsetneq {\mathcal{R}}$$\end{document}CR⊊R.
