the released survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t), computed deterministically from P via the Kaplan–Meier formula.

Theorem 22

(Phase C privacy relative to the fused plaintext and released output) Assume the threshold decryption mechanism satisfies:

(i) Threshold confidentiality: no PPT adversary holding fewer than θ secret-key shares can recover the plaintext of a well-formed ciphertext with non-negligible advantage in λ.

(ii) Share privacy: for any fixed ciphertext collection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}}$$\end{document}{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I, any strict subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}$$\end{document}CR with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$| {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}| < \theta$$\end{document}∣CR∣<θ, and the correct fused plaintext table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P={\{({n}_{i},{d}_{i})\}}_{i=1}^{L}$$\end{document}P={(ni,di)}i=1L, there exists a PPT simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{{\rm{share}}}$$\end{document}Sshare such that the simulated honest partial shares are computationally indistinguishable from the real ones:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathrm{share}}\left({\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r}\}}_{r\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}},\,{\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}},\,P\right)\,\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}\,{\left\{{\Delta }_{\iota }^{(r)}\right\}}_{r\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R},\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}}.$$\end{document}Sshare{skr}r∈CR,{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I,P≈cΔι(r)r∉CR,ι∈I.(iii) Fusion correctness: a valid threshold set of partial shares combined by the fusion algorithm yields the correct plaintext.

(iv) Deterministic partial decryption: the partial decryption algorithm is deterministic given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r},{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota })$$\end{document}(skr,ctΣ,ι).

Assumptions (ii)–(iv) are used directly in the simulation proof below. Assumption (i) is not used in the indistinguishability argument itself, but justifies interpreting the simulator input as an upper bound on the information available to any subthreshold coalition.

Then there exists a PPT simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{C}$$\end{document}SC such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{C}\,\left({\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r}\}}_{r\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}},\,{\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}},\,{\mathsf{md}},\,P,\,{\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)\right)\,\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}\,{{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(C)}(\Pi ).$$\end{document}SC{skr}r∈CR,{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I,md,P,S^HE(t)≈cViewA(C)(Π).In the all-of-R implementation, this holds for every strict subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}\subsetneq {\mathcal{R}}$$\end{document}CR⊊R, since no incomplete coalition can complete decryption independently.

Interpretation. Theorem 22 is a protocol-level reduction to the assumed security of the threshold decryption mechanism, not a derivation from RLWE alone.

Proof.

Construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{C}$$\end{document}SC as follows, given\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r}\}}_{r\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}},\,{\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}},\,{\mathsf{md}},\,P,\,{\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)).$$\end{document}({skr}r∈CR,{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I,md,P,S^HE(t)).

(a) Metadata. Copy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathsf{md}}$$\end{document}md verbatim; no signing key is needed. (b) Corrupted partial shares. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}$$\end{document}r∈CR and each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}ι∈I, compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Delta }_{\iota }^{(r)}$$\end{document}Δι(r) using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r}$$\end{document}skr and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }$$\end{document}ctΣ,ι exactly as in the real protocol. By assumption (iv), this matches the real partial shares with probability 1. (c) Honest partial shares. Invoke the share-privacy simulator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{{\rm{share}}}$$\end{document}Sshare from assumption (ii) with inputs\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r}\}}_{r\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}},\,{\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}},\,P)$$\end{document}({skr}r∈CR,{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I,P)to obtain simulated partial shares \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{\widetilde{\Delta }}_{\iota }^{(r)}\}}_{r\notin {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R},\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}}$$\end{document}{Δ~ι(r)}r∉CR,ι∈I. (d) Public outputs. Append P and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t).

We compare the simulated transcript \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{C}(\cdot )$$\end{document}SC(⋅) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(C)}(\Pi )$$\end{document}ViewA(C)(Π) component by component.

• Metadata: copied exactly, hence identically distributed.

• Corrupted partial shares: computed identically to the real protocol by assumption (iv), hence identically distributed.

• Honest partial shares: computationally indistinguishable from the real shares by share privacy, assumption (ii).

• Aggregate table and released curve: given as simulator input; in the real protocol these are computed from the ciphertexts via threshold decryption, which by fusion correctness, assumption (iii), yields the same aggregate table. The curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is a deterministic function of that table. Hence these components are identically distributed.

Any PPT distinguisher between the simulation and the real Phase C view would therefore distinguish simulated honest shares from real honest shares, violating share privacy, assumption (ii). Hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{S}}}_{C}\,\left({\{{{\mathsf{sk}}}_{r}\}}_{r\in {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}},\,{\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}},\,{\mathsf{md}},\,P,\,{\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)\right)\mathop{\approx }\limits^{c}{{\mathsf{View}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{(C)}(\Pi ).$$\end{document}SC{skr}r∈CR,{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I,md,P,S^HE(t)≈cViewA(C)(Π).

The theorem is strongest when fusion and Kaplan–Meier evaluation are performed inside the decryptor committee and only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is released externally. If the combiner is co-located with the coordinator, the coordinator transiently observes P; the theorem still characterizes the committee-member transcript while ordinary sites observe only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t). The coordinator’s transient access to the aggregate table is a potential disclosure surface and should be mitigated operationally, for example through hardened execution environments, audit logging, and access controls.

Corollary 23

(End-to-end privacy of ΠKM−Fed−CKKS: phase-level leakage summary) Under the assumptions of Theorems 20, 21, and 22, the per-phase privacy guarantees can be jointly interpreted as follows:

(i) Phase A: the setup transcript is identically distributed to a simulation that uses no honest-site plaintext data, beyond the intentionally revealed alignment grid Tall and event-time grid T.

(ii) Phase B: the encrypted aggregation transcript is computationally indistinguishable from a simulation that uses no honest-site Kaplan–Meier count vectors, beyond publicly observable metadata such as the ciphertext-index set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{I}}$$\end{document}I, ciphertext sizes, and upload timing.

(iii) Phase C: the decryption transcript of a corrupted committee member is computationally indistinguishable from a simulation that uses only the aggregate plaintext table P, the signed metadata, and the released curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t).

Proof.

Items (i)–(iii) are the direct phase-wise consequences of Theorems 20, 21, and 22. The sequential composition discussion above justifies viewing them jointly as an end-to-end phase-level leakage summary, because the following conditions hold: (1) the phases execute sequentially; (2) each phase’s public output, namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathsf{pk}},{T}_{{\rm{all}}},T)$$\end{document}(pk,Tall,T) from Phase A and the ciphertext blocks from Phase B, is the sole inter-phase communication channel; (3) corrupted parties’ secret-key shares persist as auxiliary adversary state threaded unchanged through the composition and do not affect the public inter-phase outputs; and (4) all individual-phase simulators run in PPT.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal{C}}}_{S}\cap {{\mathcal{C}}}_{R}\ne {\rm{\varnothing }}$$\end{document}CS∩CR≠∅, the shared secret-key shares appear in the adversary’s input for both Phase B and Phase C. This does not invalidate the per-phase claims: the Phase B simulator takes these shares as explicit input, and the Phase C simulator independently takes its corrupted shares as explicit input. Crucially, Theorem 22 holds for any fixed input ciphertext collection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{{{\mathsf{ct}}}_{\Sigma ,\iota }\}}_{\iota \in {\mathcal{I}}}$$\end{document}{ctΣ,ι}ι∈I, so the Phase C guarantee remains valid even when the aggregated ciphertexts are simulated outputs from Phase B rather than the real aggregates.

The above analysis establishes simulation-based privacy under a semi-honest, static adversarial model. Extending to the malicious setting would require additional mechanisms such as zero-knowledge proofs of correct encryption or range proofs compatible with CKKS approximate arithmetic, ciphertext validity checks, and verifiable threshold decryption, and is left for future work.

The guarantees are transcript-based and do not eliminate all leakage channels. Upload timing and network-layer metadata may reveal participation ordering and should be mitigated at the transport layer if needed. The plaintext time-grid union reveals the union of local timepoint sets, treated as non-sensitive in the present deployment but potentially requiring a private-set-union protocol in stricter settings. The analysis also excludes coordinator-site collusion; such collusion would enlarge the adversarial view beyond the present model, combining the coordinator’s observed ciphertexts and metadata with corrupted sites’ secret-key shares and plaintext inputs, and would require a separate analysis. Our Phase B claim, therefore, relies on Assumption 2 for the distributed CKKS layer, rather than on a standalone reduction proved in this work.

The Phase C guarantee is relative to the fused plaintext and released output, and depends on trusted execution of the threshold decryption workflow. If intermediate aggregate plaintexts are exposed outside the decryptor committee, for example due to co-location of the combiner with the coordinator, then additional trust assumptions or secure execution environments are required. These considerations highlight that the protocol provides strong cryptographic protection for local Kaplan–Meier contributions, while leaving certain system-level leakage paths and deployment choices to complementary safeguards.

IID vs. non-IID (practical invariance)

The cohort is fixed centrally, preprocessing is harmonized (units, censoring/ties), and all sites align to the common global alignment grid Tall in R1, from which the event-time grid T ⊆ Tall is derived. Under these conditions, per-time-point counts add exactly on the event-time grid (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}_{i}={\sum }_{k}{d}_{i}^{(k)},\,{n}_{i}={\sum }_{k}{n}_{i}^{(k)}$$\end{document}di=∑kdi(k),ni=∑kni(k) for ti ∈ T), so the federated KM is partition-invariant (Theorem 5): IID vs. non-IID does not change \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t). Differences arise only if harmonization or cohort assumptions are violated (section “Discussion”).

Design choices and guarantees

The number of ciphertext blocks M grows stepwise with the length of the grid used for encrypted alignment and depends on the packing layout. In the current implementation, packing is performed over Tall, so\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M}_{\mathrm{int}}=\left\lceil \frac{2|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}{B}\right\rceil \,\mathrm{and}\,{M}_{\mathrm{sep}}=2\left\lceil \frac{|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}{B}\right\rceil ,$$\end{document}Mint=⌈2|Tall|B⌉andMsep=2⌈|Tall|B⌉,which determine per-site uplink and server additions. If packing were instead performed over the event-time subset T, the same formulas would hold with ∣T∣ in place of ∣Tall∣. Let R denote the committee size (number of decryptors), where R ≤ K; experiments use R = K by default, while fixed-R variants are included for communication studies. The decryption threshold θ determines how many decryptors must participate to recover plaintext; we set θ = R by default (strict threshold).

Our protocol provides these guarantees under the stated threat model: no party learns other sites’ plaintext contributions. The coordinator sees only ciphertexts in Phase B. In Phase C, if fusion is co-located with the coordinator, it may transiently observe the aggregated plaintext count vectors (ni, di) over the aligned grid required for Kaplan–Meier evaluation; however, individual site contributions are never decrypted separately, and only the final survival curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) is released to sites, eliminating the subtraction-based residualization that is present when (di, ni) are disclosed. The computational complexity of the protocol is characterized as follows: Phase A builds Tall in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O({\sum }_{k}| {T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}| \log {\sum }_{k}| {T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}| )$$\end{document}O(∑k∣Tkall∣log∑k∣Tkall∣) time; Phase B performs O(MK) homomorphic additions with linear-in-K uplink; and Phase C performs O(RM) share generations, O(M) fusions, and O(∣Tall∣) decoding work, followed by an O(∣T∣) Kaplan–Meier pass on the event-time grid.

With the same event-time grid T and add-only aggregation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{S}}_{{\rm{HE}}}(t)$$\end{document}S^HE(t) matches the pooled oracle up to CKKS-level numerical noise; validation tolerances appear in section “Results”.

Datasets

We evaluate our approach on two datasets:

• NCCTG Lung Cancer contains 228 observations with survival times and censoring indicators (status = 1 censored, =2 event).

• Synthetic Breast Cancer (IKNL) contains 60,000 synthetic observations designed to mimic real-world survival data.

Partitioning and heterogeneity

Each dataset is horizontally partitioned among K clients. We first shuffle the full dataset (fixed seed for reproducibility) and split rows into K subsets of approximately equal size while retaining all features; this approximates an i.i.d. partition.

To study non-IID effects, we induce outcome heterogeneity via the event indicator. Before splitting we: (i) standardize the event/status column to binary event ∈ {0, 1} (map {1, 2} as 1 → 0 censored, 2 → 1 event; otherwise declared “event” values map to 1 and all others to 0); (ii) enforce strictly positive survival times (drop non-positive entries); and (iii) log configuration for reproducibility.

Let K be the number of clients. We split by event ∈ {0, 1} and allocate each group with symmetric Dirichlet weights (strong heterogeneity):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\bf{w}}}^{(g)} \sim {\rm{Dirichlet}}({\underbrace{\alpha ,\ldots ,\alpha }}_{K}),\,\alpha =0.2,\,g\in \{0,1\}.$$\end{document}w(g)~Dirichlet(α,…,α⏟K),α=0.2,g∈{0,1}.Rows in group g are randomly permuted and assigned multinomially with probabilities w(g); we then enforce a “no-empty-clients” constraint by reassigning surplus rows until every client has at least one row (this may soften the strongest skews but preserves the heterogeneity type). As α → ∞ the split tends to i.i.d.

This construction preserves the centralized multiset of rows and hence the union of survival times Tall. The event-time grid T ⊆ Tall derived from that union is therefore also preserved. At each ti ∈ T, per-client at-risk and event counts sum to centralized totals. Consequently, under exact aggregation/decryption the federated KM equals the centralized estimator, and under CKKS it converges to it with negligible numerical error (section “Theoretical Results”). For plaintext baselines we also vary client-level overlap (None/Small/Large) to analyze reconstruction risk; these overlap settings do not affect the encrypted protocol.

Evaluation protocol

We repeat each configuration 10 times to visualize curve variability, quantify numerical and statistical agreement between encrypted and pooled (oracle) pipelines, and measure overhead. We explicitly include non-IID label splits (Dirichlet α = 0.2) and overlap regimes to stress heterogeneity; as predicted by partition invariance (Theorem 5), the HE-federated Kaplan–Meier (KM) matches the pooled oracle under both IID and non-IID partitions. Data partitioning follows section “Partitioning and heterogeneity”.

Single-machine environment and timing

All experiments run on a MacBook Pro (Apple M3 Pro, 36 GB RAM, macOS Sonoma 14.5). The experiments were implemented in Python 3.12.2 using OpenFHE (C++ back end) via Python bindings. Unless stated otherwise, the reported wall-clock runtime measures the end-to-end execution of the experimental benchmark pipeline, including cryptographic setup, federated aggregation, threshold decryption, Kaplan–Meier evaluation, and associated implementation overhead. Each point shows the sample mean with 95% CIs based on the normal approximation across 10 replicates.

Homomorphic-encryption parameters

We use OpenFHE 12 multiparty (threshold) APIs for CKKS. Phase B (R2) performs slotwise additions (and occasional rotations) only; no ciphertext–ciphertext multiplications are used, so precision is governed by the initial scale (no rescaling/bootstrapping). Unless otherwise stated, experiments use the strict all-of-R setting θ = R; for fidelity and runtime studies we set R = K, while fixed-R variants are used only in communication-scaling studies.

In the current implementation, we encode at-risk counts and event counts over the full alignment grid Tall. Using two layouts:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}{\bf{\text{Interleaved :}}}\,({n}_{1},{d}_{1},{n}_{2},{d}_{2},\ldots ) & \Rightarrow & {M}_{\mathrm{int}}=\left\lceil \frac{2|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}{B}\right\rceil ,\\ {\bf{\text{Separate :}}}\,({d}_{1},\ldots ,{d}_{|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|})\,\mathrm{and}\,({n}_{1},\ldots ,{n}_{|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}) & \Rightarrow & {M}_{\mathrm{sep}}=2\left\lceil \frac{|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}|}{B}\right\rceil .\end{array}$$\end{document}Interleaved :(n1,d1,n2,d2,…)⇒Mint=⌈2|Tall|B⌉,Separate :(d1,…,d|Tall|)and(n1,…,n|Tall|)⇒Msep=2⌈|Tall|B⌉.If packing were instead performed on the event-time subset T, the same formulas would hold with ∣T∣ replacing ∣Tall∣.

With B = 8192 the stepwise dependence on the packed grid length is:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=1000:\,(1,2),\,L=5000:\,(2,2),\,L=10,000:\,(3,4)\,\,{\rm{for}}\,\,({M}_{{\rm{int}}},{M}_{{\rm{sep}}}),$$\end{document}L=1000:(1,2),L=5000:(2,2),L=10,000:(3,4)for(Mint,Msep),where L denotes the grid length used for packing. In our implementation L = ∣Tall∣. Interleaving hence gives a 2 × reduction when L ≤ B/2, ties at L ∈ (B/2, B], and a 4/3 × reduction on (B, 1.5B] (as detailed in Section S6 of the Supplementary Information).

We implement per-client uplink accounting. Encrypted payload per client in Phase B (R2) is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rm{he}}\_{\rm{uplink}}\,=\,M\times {\mathtt{ct}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}.$$\end{document}he_uplink=M×ct_bytes.Decryptor-share payload is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rm{shares}}\_{\rm{uplink}}\,=\,M\times R\times {\mathtt{share}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}.$$\end{document}shares_uplink=M×R×share_bytes.Thus total encrypted uplink to the coordinator is linear in K (sites), while share traffic is linear in R (decryptors) and independent of K.

For fidelity/statistics/runtime experiments we set R = K (all clients act as decryptors). For the communication-scalability study only, we fix R ∈ {5, 9, 25} independent of K to expose the R-linearity of share traffic. This changes communication tallies only; numerical accuracy is unaffected.

We compute restricted mean survival time (RMST(τ)) as a left-Riemann sum over the event-time grid T up to horizon τ:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rm{RMST}}(\tau )=\mathop{\sum }\limits_{{t}_{i}\le \tau }\widehat{S}({t}_{i-1})\,\Delta {t}_{i},\,\Delta {t}_{i}={t}_{i}-{t}_{i-1},\,{t}_{0}=0.$$\end{document}RMST(τ)= ∑ti≤τS^(ti−1)Δti,Δti=ti−ti−1,t0=0.Unless stated otherwise we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =\max T$$\end{document}τ=maxT.

We report two summary tolerances:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop{\max }\limits_{t\in T}\left|{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{HE}}(t)-{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{oracle}}(t)\right|\le 1{0}^{-8},\,\frac{{\sum }_{t\in T}\left|{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{HE}}(t)-{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{oracle}}(t)\right|}{{\sum }_{t\in T}\left|{\widehat{S}}_{\mathrm{oracle}}(t)\right|}\le 1{0}^{-9}.$$\end{document}maxt∈TS^HE(t)−S^oracle(t)≤10−8,∑t∈TS^HE(t)−S^oracle(t)∑t∈TS^oracle(t)≤10−9.Bands use pooled Greenwood SE (coverage and standardized sup-gap); all figures show mean with 95% CIs across the 10 repeats. Table 7 summarizes the CKKS parameter instantiation used throughout the experiments. Communication scalability

We analyze communication using closed-form counts consistent with section “Communication complexity”. Let K denote the number of sites, Tall the union of all unique survival times across sites (including censoring times), T ⊆ Tall the event-time grid used for Kaplan–Meier aggregation, R ≤ K the decryptor committee size, n the CKKS ring degree, and B = n/2 the slot capacity per ciphertext. Unless stated otherwise, payload sizes are reported in MB (decimal units, 106 bytes).

Plain protocol. Our plaintext two-round protocol operates as follows. In Round 1 (R1), each site uploads its local set of unique observed survival times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{k}^{{\rm{all}}}$$\end{document}Tkall (including both event and censoring times). The coordinator computes the union\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{\mathrm{all}}=\mathop{\bigcup }\limits_{k=1}^{K}{T}_{k}^{\mathrm{all}}$$\end{document}Tall=∪k=1KTkalland broadcasts Tall to all sites. The event-time subset T = {t ∈ Tall: ∃ an even tat t} is not constructed explicitly; it is handled implicitly because di = 0 at censoring-only times.

In Round 2 (R2), each site uploads aligned counts\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\left\{\left({n}_{i}^{(k)},{d}_{i}^{(k)}\right)\right\}}_{{t}_{i}\in {T}_{\mathrm{all}}}.$$\end{document}ni(k),di(k)ti∈Tall.

The total number of uploaded plaintext scalars is therefore\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop{\underbrace{\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}\left|{T}_{k}^{\mathrm{all}}\right|}}\limits_{{\rm{R}}1\mathrm{upload}}\,+\,\mathop{\underbrace{2K| {T}_{\mathrm{all}}| }}\limits_{{\rm{R}}2\mathrm{counts}(\mathrm{at}-\mathrm{riskandevents})}.$$\end{document}∑k=1KTkall⏟R1upload+2K∣Tall∣⏟R2counts(at−riskandevents).

The downlink consists of broadcasting Tall, giving\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}|$$\end{document}K∣Tall∣plaintext scalars in total.

The R1 uplink satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop{\sum }\limits_{k=1}^{K}\left|{T}_{k}^{\mathrm{all}}\right|\,\le \,K\left|{T}_{\mathrm{all}}\right|.$$\end{document}∑k=1KTkall≤KTall.The R1 uplink bounds (in bytes, with 8 bytes per scalar) are therefore\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\rm{uplink}}}_{\min }^{{\rm{R1}}}=| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| \cdot 8,\,{{\rm{uplink}}}_{\max }^{{\rm{R1}}}=K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| \cdot 8.$$\end{document}uplinkminR1=∣Tall∣⋅8,uplinkmaxR1=K∣Tall∣⋅8.

Combining R1 and R2 yields the min–max uplink bounds\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\rm{uplink}}}_{\min }=(| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| +2K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| )\cdot 8,\,{{\rm{uplink}}}_{\max }=(K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| +2K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| )\cdot 8=3K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| \cdot 8.$$\end{document}uplinkmin=(∣Tall∣+2K∣Tall∣)⋅8,uplinkmax=(K∣Tall∣+2K∣Tall∣)⋅8=3K∣Tall∣⋅8.

The total downlink is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K| {T}_{{\rm{all}}}| \cdot 8.$$\end{document}K∣Tall∣⋅8.

Event-time-only variant. If a deployment filters to the event-time subset T before R2, the R2 uplink reduces to 2K∣T∣ scalars; R1 communication is unchanged.

CKKS with multiparty decryption. In the encrypted protocol we retain R1 in plaintext and encrypt only R2. In the current implementation, each site encrypts two length-∣Tall∣ vectors containing at-risk and event counts aligned to the full grid Tall, with di = 0 at non-event times. With CKKS packing and slot capacity B = n/2, the number of ciphertexts per client is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=\left\{\begin{array}{ll}2\lceil | {T}_{\mathrm{all}}| /B\rceil , & \mathrm{separate}\,\mathrm{packing},\\ \lceil 2| {T}_{\mathrm{all}}| /B\rceil , & \mathrm{interleaved}\,\mathrm{packing}.\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}M=2⌈∣Tall∣/B⌉,separatepacking,⌈2∣Tall∣/B⌉,interleavedpacking.If encryption were applied only to the event-time subset T, the same formulas would hold with ∣T∣ replacing ∣Tall∣. This is not the current implementation; rather, it is an event-time-only packing variant used for the communication simulations in section “Results”, which instantiate these formulas with L = ∣T∣.

Using the conservative byte model\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathtt{ct}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}\approx \frac{2n| Q| }{8},\,{\mathtt{share}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}\approx \frac{n| Q| }{8},$$\end{document}ct_bytes≈2n∣Q∣8,share_bytes≈n∣Q∣8,the encrypted R2 uplink is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rm{HE}}\,{\rm{R2}}\,{\rm{uplink}}=KM\cdot {\mathtt{ct}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}},$$\end{document}HER2uplink=KM⋅ct_bytes,and the decryption-share traffic is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{shares}}\,{\mathrm{uplink}}=R\,M\cdot {\mathtt{share}}\_{\mathtt{bytes}}.$$\end{document}sharesuplink=RM⋅share_bytes.

Dividing by 106 converts these values to MB. Parameter grid used to instantiate the communication formulas

Assumptions. The analysis assumes: (i) payload-only accounting with transport and TLS framing omitted, (ii) R1 remains plaintext in both the plain and HE protocols, (iii) R2 uses add-only encrypted aggregation, (iv) ciphertext sizes follow the uncompressed model above, which gives a conservative upper bound, and (v) sites align on a common global alignment grid Tall, from which the event-time grid T used for Kaplan–Meier evaluation is derived.

In the current implementation, R2 uses Tall as the alignment grid passed to each client, so encrypted count vectors are packed over Tall rather than over the event-time subset T. Counts are therefore uploaded for every time in Tall, with di = 0 at non-event times. This does not change the Kaplan–Meier estimator because inserting zero-event time points refines the grid without altering the product. However, the encrypted R2 uplink in the implementation scales with M(∣Tall∣) rather than M(∣T∣). Filtering to event times before encryption would therefore reduce the number of ciphertexts whenever ∣Tall∣ > ∣T∣.
